===== 0002601_18951214_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
.` CAPITAL" CHAI.

The impatience of the general public in regard to the slow progress of events in the East is, if cur- rent rumours can be trusted, beginning to be shared by Laid Salisbury's own colleagues. An observa- tion is attributed to a member of the Cabinet—not one of the inner circle—who is supposed to share the Duke of Devonshire's views on many things that the Government are likely to cut a very poor figure when Parliament meets if they are not pre- pared with some more definite policy than Lard Salisbury appears to be pursuing at present. I can vouch for a remark made by one of the leading unofficial members of the Ministerial party to this effect :—'• If Lord Rosebery had been in power we 'would have led them a pretty dance in the same circumstances." This, I think, will be readily believed. It is easy to imagine the accusations of weakness and vacillation which would have been brought against the Liberal Government under similar conditions. However, there appears to be a general belief in Ministerial quarters that some- thing will be done before Parliament meets I hear from an authoritative source that Abdul Harnid is troubled with accesses of nervous irrit- ability which render it impossible for the Ambassa- dors to torecast for an hour what turn thesituati, n may take. It is in these bursts of ill-humour that nearly all his decisions are taken, and in the inter- vals he appears to be merely an unconcerned and impassive spectator of events.

The atrocities that take place in the compara- tively privacy of the slaughter-house are,it is said, often terrible. It is gratifying, therefore, to see that the subject has been forced upon public notice. The Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Animals, which has done so much good in certain spheres of animal suffering, has not done all that was possible in abbatoirs,and the cases which have been brought to light here and there should have sharpened its energies. As Hamlet's gravedigger sang and jested at gravemaking, so the slaughter- roan necessarily becomes callous under his daily calling; but the society can prevent callousness from becoming the grossest brutality. The evil is widespread. and now that the County Council has been urged to hold an official inquiry into the sub- ject.it is to be hoped they will do so.

There is a great rush of candidates for the posi- tion of clerk to the County Council. The salary of Str„000 a year is no doubt an inducement to many, but another attraction is the fact that the post is the greatest of its kind in the country, and one offering the greatest opportunities to a capable ad- ministrator. We hear that many of the London vestry clerks intend to be candidates, there are several applicants from the Council's staff, and a large number of town clerks throughout the coun- try have thsir eye on the position, It may be taken for granted also that there will be numerous hall- pay officers and young baristers among the appli- cants. The appointment will probably not be made before February or March, and the selection of the best man will he the most difficult as well as the most important duty which has fallen upon the new Council.

I have had the privilege of reading a very re- markable series of letters dealing with the political situation from the point of view of the Liberal leaders, just received by an energetic London Libe- ral politician. The correspondence reveals the fact that the ex-Premier and his colleagues have prac- tically determined, for tactical reasons, to preserve silence for the next few weeks. Lord Rosebery writes expressing regret that ho cannot at present speak in public; and Lord Kimberley and Lord Ripon reply to the same effect. Lord Tweedmouth states the position with much greater clearness and frankness. While anxious in every way to help the Liberal cause, he declares that in his deliberate opinion silence is best at this moment. He is per- fectly certain that in the profusion of Tory pro- mises there will be ample scope for Liberal criii- cism and attack, but until Parliament assembles Vie Liborals have no real right to challenge their opponents. When the promises are or not put into effect, then will be the time to take action. Lord Tweedmouth adds that his views may be taken to express those of the members generally of the late Government.

There was a good deal of comment on Wednesday on the indications which come from Birmingham of the attitude Mr. Chamberlain is likely to take in the coming education struggle. Satisfaction was derived from the apparently inspired announce- ment that Mr. Chamberlain will be in line with the Duke of Devonshire in opposition to their reaction- ary colleagues.

The conversion of the Thomas Riches Cambrian Colleries into a limited liability company reminds a correspondent that Mr. Riches is an old provincial Press man. He hailed originally from Norwich,and before he started money grubbing in South Wales was a reporter on the " Hereford Times," in the good or bad old days when the country reporter filled up his time at case. He was an expert com- positor. as well as phonographer Riches has not been spoilt by wealth, but retains the kindly qualities that endeared him to those who knew him as a good hearted chum far back in the fifties.

It is pleasant to note the vigour with which the : Eighty Club is carrying on its work of propaganda ' and political education. A very large number of speakers and lecturers, many of whom are drawn from the ranks of the Bar, give their gratuitous I services to the club, and are available to address meetings on a great variety of subjects not only in London,but all over the country. As showing the great and growing interest in the country on the education question, I may say that applications for speakers on this subject have been, and still are, very numerous. Under the guidance of the new secretary, Mr. W. M. Crook, a strong endeavour is being made to extend the usefulness and scope of the club, and a proposal is on foot to I.old a greater number of general meetings of the members and to institute political discussions.

Mr. Herbert Gladstone left on Wednesday on what is mere than a holiday tour, though that is what he calls it, in the East. He goes direct to AthenA, and there, joined by the Earl of Cavan, he is to go through the Southern and Eastern States of Turkey as far as Constantinople. No doubt exists that he will be received everywhere with joy by the Eastern people in the name of his father, and that he will come back loaded with information as to the dreadful atrocities committed in the Sultan's dominions. Mr. Herbert Gladstone will thus, when Parliament meets, be able to stir the hearts of the members of Parliament by an eye-witness's stories of the bloodshed that is being committed.

LADIES' LETTER.

MT DEAREST NELLIE,

This season's millinery is rather disappointing, and there is nothing very new. The beefeater hats are supposed to be a novelty, but they are- only a revival of a fashion which was in vogue a short time back. Toque shape hats are considered the smartest this winter, and are worn by both young and older ladies. Shot velvets are used extensively for trimming purposes, as well as glac6 silks; wings, feathers, quills, velvet flowers are also to be noticed. The colouring is decidedly brighter than it usually is at this time of year. We see coloured flowers, shot ribbons, and fur massed to- gether on one hat, and another blue felt shape with blue and green wings. One of the smartest shapes I have seen is the "Gentleman Joe"—one of the beefeater shape I mentioned—in black silk beaver; a low-crowned, curly-brimmed shape, like that worn by the genial "cabby" at the Prince of Wales's Theatre. The hat looks very trim and chic for wearing with a tailor-built gown, if •it has no trimming save a narrow band of thick corded ribbon round the crown, fastened in a tiny flat bow in orthodox male fashion; but it also lends itself well to more elaborate trimming, such as a panache of ostrich tips at the side, and softly curling tips on and under the brim. Then there is a lovely hat of white felt—and white 4i...1t is very popular this season—lined underneath

with black velvet, the brim being trimmed with croups of violets set at intervals right the way round, while four iridescent green wings, caught with a ' jet brooch, stand out aggressively,- and bunches of the. violets again appear beneath the brim at the back.

Velvet and plush coats are going to be in very high favour for smart wear during the winter, and the following is an entirely new and stylish design for one of these. The coat is of Lisreine must impress on you the fact that this plush is practically waterproof, and does not spot or spoil even when exposed to a sharp shower—well-fitting, and with moderately long, full basques. It is trimmed round the neck and down the front with narrow sable or skunk, though when economy has to be studied, the useful black Thibet fleece is being employed, and this costs from a shilling a yard in pite good-looking and good-wearing quality. The godet sleeves are the shape I think I have already described, and the seams outlined with narrow jet gimp. The jet ornaments for the shoulders can be obtained ready for applying, and the coat should be lined with silk, satin, or Italian cloth. The hat worn with this is quite a new shape, having the Welsh conical crown, which is peculiarly becoming to some young faces. If the shape is of silk beaver or velvet, the only trimming required is a narrow satin ribbon round the crown, secured with a paste ornament, and a group of black feathers at the side. Brown cloth is also very popular. A. smart

double-breasted coat is made in golden brown cloth with handsome braiding upon the cuffs and collar, which are also edged with sable. House dresses are frequently made with blouse bodices, although strict rulers of fashion consider them to be passé. Imagine a charming simple little toilette with a black woollen skirt, and a blouse of the same tint spotted with turquoise blue, the latter being made to fit the figure at the back, the fronts gathered at the neck and waist, beltedwith turquoise ribbons, bishop sleeves with tight wristbands to match the trimming of the collar -and waistband. Tea gowns are quite a feature just now both for afternoon and evening use. When they are not made of silk or velveteen, a pretty thin crepon, grenadine, or a figured nun's veiling may be selected. The gowns are usually loose-fitting back and front, shaped to the figure only at the sides. When they are intended literally for tea gowns, the upper portion of the bodice may be trimmed in yoke form with a hand. some passementerie or embroidery of sequins, while the sleeves should be long enough to reach the wrist. Another pretty costume is of seal- brown faced cloth; the skirt has strands and bows of seal-brown velvet on the gore seams in front.

The bisques are in seal velvet, with lovely bronze sequin•fringed passeinenterie. The vest, collar and cuffs are of pale blue chine with a black stripe.

Your loving JE.NNin.

THE EXAMINER, sATTTRD Y. DECEMBI4',R 14. 195

Potatoes in Greenland never grow larger than marble. .

The exact lenzth of our year is 385 days, 6 hours, 13 minutes and 48 6 seconds.

A racehorse g•alloping at full speed clears from twent y to twenty-four feet at every bound. Australia has a population of less than 5,000,000, and economists declare it could support 100,000,000 with ease.

The highest inhabited building in Europe is the Alpine clubhouse, on Mount Rosa—twelve thousand feet above sea level.

Irritating drugs—such as salts of arsenic, copper,zinc, and iron—should usually be given after food, except where local conditions require their administration in small doses before eating. The habit of turning round three or four times before lying down has survived in the domestic dog from his savage ancestry. It than served to break down the tall grass and make a bed. Besides her plague of rabbits, Australia is now threatened with apl ague of foxes. These animals, im- ported for the sport of fox-hunting, have increased so rapidly that a re ward is offered for their capture. The oil of tobacco found in the stems of the long-used pipes, or obtained by distillation at a red heat, is one of the most active and powerful poisons known to the chemist. A single drop will often cause death in the case of a doe or cat.

Photography as a Cure for Drunkenness. " Science Siftings" isresponsible for the following We commend this story, which has been communicated to us, to the attention of our temperance contempora- ries, "merely adding," as Mr. Richard Swiveller would Bay, "if it isn,t true, it's very good." A woman deter- mined the other day,that her husband should know how he looked when fie was drunk. She knew how he looked well enough, and needed not- that any man should tell her. Her children also knew by sad experience, but the man himself had a very Imperfect idea of the state of his case. So once, when he came home and fell into a maudlin slumber, she sent for a photographer to come forthwith, and on his arrival she set before him his work. She ordered the photographer to photogia ph her husband as he sat in the chair. The phctographer did his work, and did it well; .acd when the photograph was finished and laid beside the husband's place at breakfast it was a revelation,and -the sobered gentleman experienced a decidedly new sensation. There was no need of explanation ; the-thing explained itself. There was no chance for contradiction ; the sun tells no lies. There was no room for argument: a reform has taken place.

for her, blood was-epilt—for her, fields were won ; but, mother, never yet toiled knight in the battle- field for his lady-love as I will, in the battle of life, for my dearest lady—my own mother." She reached out per hand, and silently pressed his.

"Come, come," said Traverse—" lift up your head and smile We are young yet, both you and I! for after all you are not much older than your son I and we two will journey up and down the hills of life together—all in all te each other; and when at last we are old, as we shall be when you are seventy- seven and I am sixty—we will leave all our fortune that we shall have made to found a home for widows and orphans,—as we were, and we will pass out and go to Heaven together."

Now indeed this poor, modern Hagar looked— and smiled at the oddity of her Ishmael's far- reaching thought. In that poor household grief might not be Indidged. Marah Rocke took down her work-basket and sat down to finish a lot of shirts, and Traverse went out with his horse and saw, to look for a job at cutting wood for twenty-five cents a cord. Small beginnings of the fortune that was to found and endow asylums! but many a fortune has been com- menced upon less!

Marah 'Rock° had managed to dismiss her boy with a smile—but that was the last effort of nature; as soon as he was gone and she found herself alone, tear after tear welled up in her eyes and rolled down her pale cheeks; sigh after sigh heaved her bosom

Ah ; the transitory joy of the past week had been but the lightning's arrowy course accithing where it illumined I

She felt as if this last blow, that had struck her down from the height of hope to the depth of despair, had broken her heart—as if the power of reaction was gone, and she mourned as one who would not be comforted. _ _ _

While she sat thus the door opened, and before she was aware of his rresence, Herbert Greyson entered the room and came softly to her side. Ere she could speak to him, he dropped upon one knee at her feet, bowed his young head lowly over the hand that he took and pressed to his lips. Then he arose and stood before her. This was not not un- natural or exaggerated—it was his way of expres- sing the reverential sympathy and compassion he felt for her strange, life-long m„T,yrdom. "Herbert, you here? why we only got your letter this morning," she said, in tones of gentle inqu,ry, as she arose and placed a chair for him. "Yes, I could not bear to stay away from you, at such a time; I came up in the same mail coach that Eraught my letter ; but I kept myself out of Traverse's sight, for I could not bear to intrude upon you, in the first hour of your disappointment," said He raert, in a roken voice.

"Oh I that need not have kept you away, dear boy; I di, t not cry much; I am used to trouble, you know; I shall get over this also—after a little while —and things will go on in the old way," said 111,, rah Roc :e, struggling to repress the rising emotion that however overcame her, for cropping her head upon her "sailor boy's" shoulder, she burst into a flood of ter i and wept plenteously. "Dear mother, be comforted," he said; "dear mother, be comforted."

CHAPTER XIII. ARAH'S 'MEMORIES. In the shade of the apple tree again She saw a rider draw his rein. And gazing down with a timid grace, She felt his pleased eyes read her face." —WHITTIER.

"Dear Marah, I cannot understand your strong attachment to that bronzed and grlzzled old man, who has besides treated you so barbarously," said Herbert.

"Is he bronzed and gray?" asked Marah, looking up with gentle pity in her eyes and tone. "Why, of course he is. He is sixty-three." "Ha was forty-five when I first knew him, and he was very handsome then—at least I thought him the very perfection of manly strength, and beauty, and goodness. True, it was the mature, warm beauty of the Indian summer—for he was mcre than middle-aged; but it was very genial to the chilly, loveless morning of my early life,' said Marah dropping her head upon her hand, and sliding into reminiscences of the past. "Dear Marah, I wish you would tell me all about your marr.age and misfortunes," said Herbert, in a tone of the deepest sympathy and respect. "Yes, he was very handsome," continued Mrs. Rocke, speaking more to herself than to her com- panion; "his form was tall, full, and stately ; his complexion warm, rich, and glowing; his fine face was lighted up by a pair of strong, dark grey eyes, full of fire and tenderness, and was surrounded by waving masses of jet black hair and whiskers—they are grey now, you say, Herbert ?"

"worey and' grizzled, and bristling up around his hard face, like thorn-bushes around a rock ia winter I" said Herbert, bluntly, for it enraged his honest but inexperienced boyish heart to hear this wronged woman speak so enthusiastically. "Ahi it is winter with him now, but then it was glorious Indian summer. He was a handsome, strong and ardent man. I was a young, slight, pale girl, with no beauty but the cold and colour- less beauty of a statue; with no learning, but such as I had picked up from a country school; with no love to bless my lonely life—for I was a friendless orphan, without either parents or relatives, and living by sufferance in a cold and loveless home."

" Poor girl I" murmured Herbert, in almost in- audible tones. "Our log-cabin stood beside the military road leading through the wilderness to the Fort where he was stationed. And oh, when he came riding by each day, upon his noble, coal black steed, and in his martial uniform, looking so vigorous, handsome and kingly, he seemed to me almost a god of wor- ship. Sometimes be drew rein in front of the old oak tree that stood in front of our cabin, to breathe his horse, or to ask for a draught of water. I used to bring it to him. Oh I then, when he looked at me, his eyes seemed to send new warmth to ray chilled heart; when he spoke, too, his tones seemed to strengthen me; while he staid, his presence seemed to protect me."

" • Ay, such protection as vultures give to doves— covering and devouring them,'" muttered Herbert to himself. Mrs. Hooke, too absorbed in her reminiscences to heed his interruptions, continued:

"One day he asked me to be his wife. Ido not know what I answered, or if I answered anything. I °Lily know that when I understood what he meant my heart trembled with instinctive terror at Its own excessive joy I We were privately married by the chaplain at the Fort. There were no accommodations for the wives of officers there. And besides, my husband did not wish to announce our marriage, until be was ready to take me to his princely mansion in Virginia." "Humph I " grunted Herbert, inwardly, for com- ment.

"But he built for me a pretty cabin in the woods below the fort, furnished it simply, and hired a half-breed Indian woman to wait on me. Oh, I was too happy I To my wintry spring of life summer had come, warm, rich and beautiful! There is a clause in the marriage service which enjoins the husband to cherish his wife. I do not believe many people ever stop to think how much is in that word. He did; he cherished my little thin, chill, feeble life, until I became strong, warm and health- ful. Oh! even as the blessed sun warms and animates, and glorifies the earth, causing it to brighten witk life, and blossom with flowers, and bloom with fruit, so did my husband enrich, and cherish, and bless my life. Such happiness could Dot and it did not last."

Butter as a Substitute for Medicine.

It is one of the problems which constantly have to be solved by our doctors, how to supply sufficient oily food tee lubricate the human machine, to which it is an abscr late necessity, especially in winter. Young people (growing qulckly), nervous invalids, all who suffer from wasting diseases (such as influenza), are often con- demned to a course of cod-liver oil, which, however, seldom agrees with a weak digestion. Our medical men have lately Lit upon the pleasant fact that butter is almost, if not quite as nourishing as cod-liver oil and as it is much easier to assimilate, larges doses can he given with impunity. A quarter of a pound of good butter spread upon thin slices of bread, can be taken with ease in the day by a patient who cannot digest cod-liver oil, and is now ordered with the best results. where exhausting illness has reduced the body,or quick growth needs extra nourishment. This is good news both for the farmers, the patients and the children, who will no longer be fed upon " bread and scrape," for they specially need plenty of good butter.

Meat Versus Vegetables..

Dr. Mortimer Granville in did "National," for this month, puts forward his "New Theory of Gout." Eat , plenty of meat; or, strictly, " let the diet be as nearly as possible that of a meat-feeding animal." So shall you avoid gout. He adds :—The meat-eating people have prospered, even in adversity. They have been clever and successful. The vegetable-feeders, on the othEr hand, have failed. They have been effeminate feeble- minded, and devoid of intellectual force add character. Are we not at this moment witnessing the cruelly, corruptibility, vacillation and folly, and are we not anticipating the collapse of the greatest meat-scorning and teetotal nationality which disgraces modern civilisa- tion? Man is a cooking and brewing animal, and his proper food consists largely of meat, with a due amount of vegetables, and a sufficient quantity of alrohol. Unless this be recognised, we too, borne down by the unsound advice of our unphysiological" doctors," of our "total abstinence" prophets, and of the more feeble,but mischievous, advocates of a diet ef roots and herbs, shall degenerate into a race of nonentities, fall out of our place in the :struggle for existence, anti languish and decay.

The Effect of Fine Work on The Eyes. One of the first prohibitions of the oculist is the needle " You must give up fine sewing and fancy work," he says, "or your eyes will be ruined." Why the modern eye and the eye of cultivation and viod society is so much more difficult to deal with than the eye of the peasant and the women of old is a question that most people seem never to have given thought to. The Mexican, South American, Irish, and German women do the finest of drawn work and embroidery long after their feeble and tottering limbs refuse to support them. They begin this work almost as soon as they are out of their cradles, and continue it through a long course of years. Thetnodern fashionable woman, el- pecially in this country, does very little fancy work until she has nearly reached her majority, and then, or perhaps long before then, she goes intolhe hands of the oculist, and remains there most of the time during her natural life. She suffers tortures, and there must be some reason for this, and the wonder is that specialists in the treatment of eyes have not published to the world one of the prime causes of all of this trouble. The diffi- culty lies in the extremely bad light in which people work. Window draperies, awnings,and shutters are the bane of the eyesight. Tinted furniture that the sun would spoil, is preferred to soundness of vision,'although those who barter these priceless possessions for such taw- dry wares do so without the faintest idea of the ala of which they are guilty. Women with troublesome eyes seem to have a mania for badly-lighted rooms. Some recent experiments have demonstrated that if persons thus afflicted will take their work into the broad light of the day, sunshine if possible, so long as the work does not glisten nor glitter, they will have very much legs trouble. The theory seems to be that the eye naturally contracts a little in bright light,-and no strain whatever is required on the nerves. On the ,contrary, in a dim light there is a continual effort, and consequent danger of overtaxing. It is. iinportant that tlui light fall en the work and net on the eyes, and-even much more import- ant that the light come from above, and that there is no refleotion'vihatever. Of all conditions to be avoided, this is the most imperative. Reflected light has ruined more eyes than almost anything, except dark rooms. If one is doing work out of doors or at a window where there is any reflection, it is well to throw a dark cloth over a screen on the side from which the reflection comes. Indeed, the judiciously-arranged screen, with dark dra- pery, is of the utmost importance if one would preserve the eyesight unimpaired.

CHAPTER XII. MARAH'S DREAMS. "And now her narrow kitchen walls Stretched sway into stately halls; The weary wheel to a spinnet turned, The tallow candle an astral burned; A manly form at her side she saw, And joy was duty and love was Iaw."—WHITTIKS.

On the same Saturday morning that Herbert Grey- son hurried away from his friend's cottage, to travel post to Hurricane Hall, for the sole purpose of ac- celerating the coming of her good fortune, Marah Rocke walked about the house with a step so light, with eyes so bright, and cheeks so blooraing, that one might have thought that years had rolled back- ward in their course and made her a young girl again!

'Traverse gazed upon her in delight. Reversing the words of the text, he said:

'We must call you no longer Marsh, (which is bitter,) but we must call you Naomi, (which is beautiful,) mother!"

"Young flatterer :" she answered, smiling and slightly flushing. "But tell me truly, Traverse, am I very much faded? have care, and toil, and grief made me look old r

"Youl old 1" exclaimed the boy, running his eyes over her beaming face and graceful form with a non-comprehension that might have satisfied her, but did not, for she immediately repeated: "Yes, do I look old? Indeed, Ido not ask from sanity, child I Ab, it little becomes me to be vain; but I do wish to look well in some one s eyes!" " I wish there was a looking-glass in the house, mother, that it might tell you, you should be called Naomi, instead of Marah 1"

"Ah 1 that is just what he used to say to me in the old happy time,—the time in Paradise, before the serpent .entered l"

•"Wfutt ' he,' mother ?" "Your father, boy, of course I"

"Mother, will you tell me about my father, and also what it is beyond this promise kindness of Major Warfield that has made you so happy!" he asked.

"Not now, my boy dear boy, not now! I must not, I cannot, I dare not yet! Wait a few days and you shall know all! Oh, it is hard to keep a secret from my boy but then it is not only my secret, but another's! You do not think hard of me for with. holding it now, do you Traverse 1" she asked, affectionately. "No, dear mother, of course I don't. I know you must be right, and I am glad to see you happy." "Happy! Oh, boy, you don't know how happy I am! I did not think may human being could ever feel so joyful in this erring world, much less I! One cause of this excess of joyful feeling must be from the contrast! else it were dreadful to be so happy 1" "Mother, I don't know what you mean," said Traverse, uneasily, for he was too young to under- stand these paradoxes of feeling and thought, and there were moments when he feared for his mother's MEM.

"Oh, Traverse, think of it 1 eighteen long, long years of estrangement, sorrow, and dreadful suspense! eighteen long, long, weary years of patience against anger, and loving against hatred, and hoping against despair I your young mind cannot grasp it—your very life is not so long. I was seventeen then; I am thirty-five now. And after wasting all my young years of womanhood in loving, hoping, long. lug—lot the light of life has dawned at last." "God save you, mother!" said the boy, fervently, for her wild, unnatural joy continued to augment his anxiety. "Ah, Traverse, I dare not tell you the secret now, and yet I ant always letting it out; because my heart overflows from its fulness. Ab, boy, many, many weary nights have I lain awake from grief; but last night I lay awake from joy. Think 0 it."

The boy's only reply to this was a deep sigh. He was becoming seriously alarmed. _ . .

- t "1 never s..aw her so excited. I wish she would get calm," was his secret thought. Then, with the design of changing the current of her ideas, he took off his coat, and said: "Mother, my pocket is half torn out, and though , there's no banger of my losing a great deal out of lilt, still I'll get you, please, to sew it in while I mend the fence."

"Sew the pocket! mend the fence! Well," smiled Mrs. Backe, "we'll do so, if it will amuse you. The mended fence will be a convenience to the next tenant, and the patched coat will do for some poor boy. Ah, Traverse, we must be very good to the poor, in more ways than in giving them what we do not ourselves need, for we shall know what it is to have been poor," she concluded, In more serious tones than she had yet used.

Traverse was glad of this, and went out to his work feeling somewhat better satisfied.

This delirium of happiness lasted intermittently a whole week, during the last three days of which Mrs. Rocke was constantly going to the door and looking up the road, as if expecting some one. The mail came from Tip-Top to Staunton only once a week, on Saturday mornings. Therefore, when Saturday came again, she sent her son to the post office, saying: "If they do not come to-day, they will surely write."

Traverse hastened with all his speed, and got there so soon that he had to wait for the mail to be opened. Meanwhile, at home, the widow walked the floor in restless, joyous anticipation, or went to the door and strained her eves up the road to watch for Tra- verse, and perhaps for some one else's coming. At last she discerned her son, who came down the road, walking rapidly, smiling triumphantly, and holding ' a letter up to view. _

She ran out of the gate to meet him, seized and kissed the letter, and then, with her face burning, her heart palpitating, and her fingers trembling, she hastened into the house, threw herself into the little low chair by the fire, and opened the letter. It was from Herbert, and read thus:

Hurricane Hall, Nov. 30th, 1843. My dearest and best Mrs. Rockc May God strengthen you to read the few bitter lines I have to write. Most unhappily, Major Warfield did not know exactly who you were, when he promised so much. Upon learning your name he withdrew all his promises. At night, in his library, he told me all your early history. Having heard all, the very worst, I believe you as pure as an angel. So I told him. So I would uphold with my life, and seal with my death. Trust yet in God, and believe in the earnest respect and affection of your grateful and attached son, lIERI3ERT GREYSON. , P.B.—For henceforth I shall call you mother. Quietly she finished reading, pressed the letter again to her lips, reached it to the fire, saw it, like her hopes, shrivel up to ashes, and then she arose, and with her trembling fingers clinging together, walked up and down the floor. There were no tears in her eyes, but oh, such a look of unutterable woe on her pale, blank, despairing face. Traverse watched her, and saw that something had gone frightfully wrong; that some awful revolution of fate or revulsion of feeling had passed over her in this dread hour.

_ Cautiously he approached her, gently he laid his band upon her shoulder, tenderly he whispered: "Mother I" She turned and looked strangely at him, then exclaiming: "Oh, Traverse, how happy I was this day week 1" she burst into a flood of tears.

Traverse threw his arm around his mother's waist, and half-coaxed and half-bore her to her low chair, and sat her in it, and knelt by her side; and embracing her fondly, whispered: "Mother, don't weep so bitterly. You have me, am I nothing? Mother, / love you more than son ever loved his mother, or suitor his sweetheart, or husband his wife. Oh, is my love nothing, mother 7 "

Oniy sobs answered him. "Mother," he pleaded, "you are all the world to tne—let me be all the world to you. I can be it, mother,-1 can be it; try me. I will make every effort for my mother, and the Lord will bless us." Still no answer but convulsive sobs. "Oh, mother, mother, I will try to do for you more than ever son did for mother, or man for woman before, dear mother, if you will not break my heart by weeping so."

The sobbing abated a little, partly from exhaus- tion and partly from the soothing influences of the boy's loving words.

"To be where I could sometimes hear of my husband, without intruding on him. When she died I cherished you, Herbert, first because you were his nephew, but now, dear boy, for your own sake, also." "And I. while I live, will be a son to you, Madam l I will be your constant friend at Hurricane Hall. He talks of making me his heir. Should he persist in such blind injustice, the day I come into the property, I shall turn it all over to his widow and son. But Ido not believe that he will persist; I, for my part, still hope for the best." "I also hope for the best, for whatever God wills Is sure to happen, and his will is surely the best I Yes, Herbert, I also hope—beyond the grave!" said Marsh Rock% with a wan smile.

The little clock that stood between the tall plated candlestieks on the mantel-piece struck twelve. and Tilarah rose_ from her amt.

"Traverse, poor fellow, will he home to hils dinner. Not a word to him, Herbert, please I I do not wish the poor lad to know how much he hall lost, and above all, I do not wish him to be prejudiced against his father." "You are right, Marsh," said Herbert, "for if he were told, the natural indignation that your wrongs would arouse in his heart, would totally unfit him] to meet his father, in a proper spirit, in that even' fOX which I will hope—a future and a perfect family Union 1".. tg• * - * Herbert Greyson remained a week with hit friends, during which time he paid the quarter's rent, and relieved his adopted mother of that cause of anxiety. Then he took leave and departed for Hurricane Hall, on his way to Washington City, whence he was immediately going to pass hir examination and await his appointment.

RATS!

The golden city of Ballarat is said to be the only city in the world which can claim perfect immunity from rats. Not so Birmingham ;it is reported that two res- taurant-keepers have had to file petitions in bankruptcy in consequence of the serious damage done to their stock by rats. Tha famous Midland town is, it seems, suffer- ing from a plagueof rats, and the principal simpkeepere complain very seriously of the losses they are sustain- ing. A rather good rat story is told by a French con- temporary of an incident which recently occurred at .a town in the South of France during the Christmas visit of a circus. One of the chief attractions of the show was a troupe of performing dogs, and after they had gone through various feats, their trainer otQserved that Azor, the most accompdshed of them all, would favour the audience with a pianoforte solo. Accordingly Azor mounted a chair and struck up the " Marseillaise." At this moment, however, someone in the audience shouted out " Rats ! " Azor made one bound in the direction of the cry; but the pianoforte went on playing.

THE MORECAMBE PIER DISASTER.

CLAIMS FOR DAMAGES: FIFTEEN ACTIONS. At the Leeds Town Hall on Monday, the Under Sheriff (Mr. E. Gray) and a jury, sat to assess damages in fifteen actions which had been brought against the Morecambe Pier Company, Limited, in respect of yie disaster which occurred by the collapse of the landing jetty on the 19th of September last, when two persona were drowned and forty others immersed in the sea, several of the latter being severely injured. In the first five cases Mr. Waddy, Q.C., and Sir George Morrison appeared for the claimants ; in the whole of the cases Mr. Overend Evans, of Southport, represented the com- pany. The first case taken was that of William Illing- worth,a miner, of Methley,who sued under Lord Cann, - bell's Act for damages for himself and son for the loss of his wifo.—Mr.Waddy said that the company had con- structcd at the end of the pier a landing stage tor plea- sure steamers plying between Morecambe and Black- pool, and other seaside resorts, the company chargipg id. per passenger, and thus making a considerable in- come. Consequently they could well afford to make a perfectly safe structure. The directors,however,seemed to think that any ramshackle kind of thing would do, and the landing stage was absolutely unfitted for the Rig - pose. On the 9th Se2tember the plaintiff,his wife,chlld. and sister-in-law went for a holiday to Blackpool and back by the steamer Express, commanded by Captain Thompson. When the steamer came alongside the land- ing stage the structure collapsed, and the whole of. the persons upon it were thrown into the sea. Plaintiff's wife was drowned, his boy was with difficulty saved by..' Captain Thompson, and he himself was seriously injured by the falling ironwork.—For the company Mr. Evana denied that the structure was a ramshackle one, or that it had been condemned.—The jury awarded Illingworth and his boy £lOO each for the loss of his wife, and £4O to himself for injuries to his shoulder. WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT MARE

AND FOAL ASSOCIATION.

MEETING OF SUBSCRIBERS.

A general meeting of the subscribers of the above society was held at 23, Bold-St., Warrington, on Monday afternoon, the chair being occupied by Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart. There were also present—Messrs. John White, J.P., H. Mawdsley, Joseph Shakeshaft, William Richardson, Caldwell, Lawton, H. Greenough, Edward Gibbin, Colin Mclver (hon. sec.), and H. B. Melville (assistant hon. sec.) The HON. SECRETARY read a letter from Mr. F. Monk, J.P., regretting his inability to attend the meeting, and stating that if there should be any wish expressed to ' re-elect him as vice-president he (the secretary) would oblige by informing those present that he could not ac- cept that office again. The resignation was accepted. The Hon. Secretary then read the annual report, which stated that the society this year had been indebted to Lady Greenall for the valuable privilege of holding the show in Walton Park, and that contributed in no small degree to the success of their show. Sir Gilbert Greenall entertained with his wonted hospitality, at luncheon, all the exhibitors and many other friends and visitors. He also added to the attraction of the show by having secured the attendance of the Walton Brass Band,and by kindly admitting the public to the stables, Sm. The showyard fittings were put up on the usual terms be- tween Sir Gilbert Greenall and the committee, an ar- rangement which was exceedingly favourable to the society. The society was now in possession of a per- manent show-ring fence, free of expense. For this they were indebted to Mr. R. A. Naylor,who kindly presented the society with the pssts and rails ready for fixing. It had been a most useful and acceptable present, and would save much annual expense. The exhibits that year were better in quality than the previous year in the shire classes, but the entries in the. hunter classes did not show the improvement either in number or in quality that he should liked to have seen. The experi- ment of having had an open class for shire mares and foals was a successful one,and should be continued in his opinion, at future shows. It might be as well perhaps, in future, ts exclude from any of the society's classes any animal that had taken a first prize at a county show in the same year. The draught horses were ex- cellent in quality, and he hoped would increase in num- ber at their next show. The turnouts were a decided improvement on last year's exhibits. The judges, assisted by very able and zealous stewards, got through their duties in good time, and he believed tffeir decisions were received with satissaction by tne exhibitors. The classes were got in and out of the ring with promptness by the stewards, and it was most pleasing to have had such untiring members to work for the society. Before closing his report he wished tc put before them one fact, that there appeared to be among their subscri- bers and exhibitors a wish to extend their show so as to include cattle and agricultural produce. The question was worthy of consideration, but in his opinion the cattle should be kept to breeding animals only. The object of the society was to induce members to become the breeders as well as the exhibitors or their stock of all descriptions. If anything was to be done in the direc- tion of extending the show, their members should have some intimation of the fact that winter, so as to enable them to select suitable heifers, 86. c., for the purpose. The report gave a table showing the results of the show for the last five years. In 1891 the gate money taken amounted to £33 125., and in 1895 it had fallen to £33 Is. 3d. In 1893 it realized £37 53. 6d. The total receipts in 1891 were £65 18s. 3d., and in 1895 £lOO 16s. 6d. The attendance in 1893 Was 1,426, in 1894 1,120, and in 1895 1,900.—Mr. JOHN WHITE moved the adoption of the report, and that the special attention of the committee be called to the proposed extension.— I This was seconded by Mr. GREENOUGH, and carried. The Hon. SECRETARY said that if the proposed enlarge- I ment was to be carried out there must of necessity be a re-arrangement of the committee,officials, and staff. It was very desirable the committee should have a tree hand. It had been a great pleasure to himself and to Mr. Melville to have taken part in the work of the society during the past few years, and is was to be hoped that the day was not far distant when the show would be recognized as the leading one in that important agricul- tural district. (Hear, hear.) Neither he not Mr. Mel- ville could see his way to become responsible as secre- taries for the details of the show, as they,were now some- what numerous.—The CHAIRMAN: It has not been de- finitely decided that the show shall be enlarged.—Mr. MAWDSLEY The show seems to be at a standstill. It seems to take it all its time to go on as it does.—Mr. GiaxEstobos : If you were to give a prize for jumpers you would get more gate money. We have to cater for the public. If we go on as we have been doing during the past three or four years the sooner the show is shut up the better. We "not something besides a few colts and rnares.—Mr. MAWDSLEY When the society was formed the object in view was to encourage breeding—it is a breeding society.—The CHAIRMAN : We are not formed to cater for the public.—Mr. MAWDSLEY : And a great deal of good has been done in the matter of breed- ing. (Hear, hear.)—Mr. Joni; Warm : It would be a good thing if we could give encourage- ment to farmers to keep a few more cows. There are very few in this part of Cheshire now.— A suggestion was made that implements should be in. troduced into the show yard, but it was not fav- ourably received.—Sir GILBERT GREENALL than pro- posed that the show be enlarged for the introduction of cattle and agricultural produce, and that the executive Committee prepare a scheme to be presented at the an- nual meeting in February,including necessary rules and prize list.—Mr. WILLIAM RICHARDSON seconded.—Mr. GREENOUGH repeated his statement that prizes for jumpers would attract many people to the show.—The CHAIRMAN : We don't want the people, we want to im- prove the breed of animals.—Mr. JOHN WHITE If you introduced jumpers you would have to erect a grand atand.—Mr. GREENOUGH: Where one would go to see a cow, 20 would go to see a horse jump over a fence. The Warrington show ought to be as good as the Prescot show.—The CHAIRMAN: It is a better show to-day.-- Mr. Gass/moon : In some respects it is.—The question relating to the secretaries was referred to the Executive Committee, who will advise what steps should be taken at the annual meeting. A hope was expressed that in view of the valuable services which: bad been rendered by Messrs. Mclver and Melville an effort would be Made M induce them to remain in office.

A. WOMAN SENTENCED TO DEATH.

AN ACCOMPLICE ARRESTED IN COURT.

At the Warwick ASSIZ9S on Tuesday, Sarah Ann Eden, a midwife, was indicted for the murder of Rebecca Simister, a married woman, at Aston, it being alleged that she had performed an illegal operation which had proved fatal. Before her death Mrs. Simister told her husband what had been done, and it was asserted that the prisoner then begged him not to tell the doctors who had been called to attend Mrs. Simister. The prisoner was found guilty and sentenced to death. The Judge remarked that the crime was only too common. He hoped the sentence would deter others from pursuing this wicked trade. The same prisoner and Matilda Man- ning, dressmaker,of Aston, were jointly indicted for the manslaughter of Mabel Gordon, a barmaid, of Leeds, on the 28th of October, by performing an illegal operation. Eden was, however, not put upon her trial in this case. The principal witness was John Hineson, commercial traveller, of Hull, who said he had been intimate with the deceased woman Mabel Gordon, and in September she informed him of her condition. He made arrange- ments for her to go to Manning's house at Aston. He had written a letter to Manning with respect to Mabel Gordon going to her house, but he had destroyed the letters which he had received in reply to his letters. He admitted writing a letter to the prisoner beginning " My darling Tilly," in which he said : "You ask me about Miss Gordon knowing the relations between us. She knew nothing when I wrote, but I have bad since to tell her,but even then not too much. That is why I ad- dressed you as I did." In another letter witness ad- mitted writing "It' all is over before Clarice goes to the Shop in the morning, send me a wire saying how things have gone on. I got your letter this morning, and from information received I think the party in question has somewhere about ES for the job. You therefore will know how to act." The Judge asked witness what he meant by asking for a wire if all was over, and witness said he meant if Manning had met Gordon. The Judge said he expected an intelligent answer to his question, but the witaess need not incriminate himself. In a further letter the witness admitted writing : " Gordon Can go back on Thursday. She will be very foolish to go before,and run a great risk of taking cold." Mr. Hugo Young had commenced his cross-examination of Hineson when the foreman of the jury was seized with a fit,and had to he removed from the court under medical attendance. During the enforced stoppage of the case the Judge held a consultation with Mr. Soden, the lead- ing counsel for the prosecution, whereupon Mr. Soden announced that he did not intend to proceed further with the case against the prisoner and would consent to an acquittal.—The Judge said he fully agreed with the de- cision of the prosecution. It appeared to him that there had been a misplacing of people in the dock. The pri- soner had been very properly charged with manslaughter and stood a great risk of being convicted,but under the circumstances which had transpired he could not have passed a proper sentence upon her if convicted. He hoped the police would take good care that the witness in the box took up the position which the woman occu- pied, for it was quite clear that he was the person who ought to be put upon the trial for this crime. —The police then arrested the witness Hineson for the man- slaughter of Mabel Gordon.

VA COINATIO

"A Local Physician, who enjoys a large practice not ar from here, nearly lost one of his best families the other day through Vaccination,and you will do well to be very careful lest you encounter similarexperiA,ces." The man who thus addressed me was one of my neighbours, and a most successful doctor himself. "I am breaking no rules of etiquette," lie added, "in telling you of this, as I happen to be the doctor referred to. and, besides, I shall not disclose the name of the family. My experi- ence also will interest you, and, I think, instruct you." "Be brief," I said, "for I have heard of many a quarrel and many an illness through Vaccination.' " Three months ago," said he, " MieS Evelyn-- . . .

, called upon me and wished to be vaccinated. As she was of age, and I was their regular family physician. I asked no questions, but did as she desired. Next day I received a call from her mother, who informed me that their first child, a beautiful boy, had died from vaccination,and both she and her husband were greatly annoyed and worried over Evelyn, and wished me to prescribe for her at once. I assured her the lymph I had used was pure,and could do no harm, and that no remedy could be given at that stage which would be of any value. She left me very dissatisfied. Two weeks later mother and daughter called, the mother furious and the daughter in tears. The poor girl's face was utterly disfigured with blotches, pimples, and redness, and her mother told me sh,; had several similar marks upon her body. You can understand my position. I ' knew of no remedy to prescribe, and I dreaded the re- sults. My explanation that I could not understand it gave but little comfort. I felt I must prescribe , something, and so I wrote one ot our ordinary, simple, and, I may add, useless prescriptions, cheered them up, said ' Good Morning," and timed to my desk in a state . of positive distraction. As I turnee my eye fell upon I the letters V.V.V.' which were printed upon the cover of a pamphlet that I had, two hours before, with a sense of annoyance, thrown into the waste paper basket. I picked it up, glanced over the first few lines, and turned quickly towards the door through which Evelyn and her mether were just disappearing. ' I'll call to-morrow,' was all I said. V.V.V.. or Vaccination Virus Vanquisher, occupied my attention most of that night. I read and re-read every word contained in the pamphlet. I said to myself, If this he true, and it certainly appears to be,l have found a s:..ientific remedy of the greatest value.' As you know, I am familiar with the:analysis of drugs, so I decided to thoroughly investigate the remedy before pre- scribing it. I purchased a bottle from a chemist who did not know me, as I did not wish my chemist to see me buying what appeared to be a patent medicine. I spent hours in analysing,or rather trying to analyse, the medicine,but found that it contained an antiseptic which could not be broken up,a most important point,and which though powerful,was very mild. SATURDAY EVENING CONCERTS AT PAR it HALL.

The first of a series of Saturday evening concerts to be held at Parr Hall, Warricgton, was given on Satur- day evening. The attendance, although not so large as the concert merited, was not discouraging, but if the excellence of last Saturday's concert is to be maintained, a full house will be necessary. Amongst those present may be mentioned the Mayor (Councillor James Fair- dough), with members of the Town Council, and officials. The scenery, which had been erected for the occasien, although not extensive or luxurious, was very effective, and gave evidence—if any were needed—of the adaptability of the place for a theatrical perform- ance. With the exception of the items contributed by the Warrington Borough Band, the whole of the pro- gramme was provided by artistes from other places. " Torquato Tasso " by thy band served as an overture. The performanle, although not altogether fault- less in execution, was highly creditable band, and it was rather unfortunate for them that one of the newly fixed flies should catch fire during their performance,ancl thus divert attention. The first part of the programme was of a miscellaneous character, and was taken part in by the following artistes :—Miss Fanny Be Bouffiers (soprano), miss 11.:,5e Keefe (mezzo•soprano), Mr. George Barton (tenor) , Mr. Sidney Taylor (tenor) , Mr. Alex Wood (baritone), Mr. Wilfred Elli- son (basso), and Mr. Wilson Blackledge (humorist). a trio, entitled " The Mariners " (J. Naviganti) taken part in by Miss De Bouffiers Mr. Sidney Taylor, and Mr. Wood,was particularly fine, there being a pleasing, harmonius blending of voices. Two very familiar songs were sung by Mr. Barton, viz., " The Sailor's Grave," and " The Holy City," which were rendered in a manner that made apparent their iatrinsic merit, Needless to say be was vociferously recalled and gave as an encore "My Sweetheart when a Boy." Miss Fanny De Bouffiers was similarly successful in gaining the goodwill of the audi- ence by her singing of the vocal waltz " Il Baccio," a niece abounding in difficult though delightful passages. In response to continued applause, she complied with a song, certainly better understood by the audience, en- titled "Coming through the Rye," and she sung it with that sparkling vivacity which showed how fully she had caught the meaning of the words. Miss Rose Keefe gave an intelligent rendering of the song" Love smiles but to deceive," and Mr. Wilfred Ellisen treated us with his rich bass voice with a capital rendering of "The Village Blacksmith." In response to the loud and con- tinued applause of the audience, he twice bowed his acknowledgments. Mr. Wilson Blackledge, of St. Helens, has already gained for himself a high reputation as a hismorist,and he certainly maintained it on Satur- day. In addition to his sketches being given in an ex- ceedingly clever manner he can also claim them as being of his own invention. His mimicry of pedlars, street musicians, and singing in general, attested his ability to rapidly change the time of his voice, and with good effect. His performance greatly pleased the audi- ence, and lie was frequently applauded. For the second ' part, a costume recital of Wallace's famous and well- known opera " Maritana " was given. Many will be familiar with it but a few lines of the argument may be here gives. The scene is laid in Madrid, and whilst singing te a crowd of people in a square of this ancient city, " Maritana" (a handsome (jitana) attracts the admiration of the King. This is observed by an unscru- pulous courtier named Don Jose, and he determines to satisfy the King's whim, and afterwards to betray him to the Queen, with whom he is bold enough to be in love. For the purpose of carryine, out his plans, Don Jose persuades an impetuous spendthrift Don Caesar de Bazan,who is about to be hanged,to marry a veiled lady, I and for so doing, his sentence is changed to the more soldier-like death of being shot. The veiled wife, who is none other than Maritana, is introduced to the King as her husband, Count de Bazan, whom she is told she has just married. Unfortunately for Don Jose's scheme, the real Count was not shot, and escaping from prison, he forces his way into the room, just as Maritana leaves the King, who had continued to pursue her with his unwel- come attentions. Count Cresar proves to Maritana that he is the man to whom she was so mysteriously wedded, and on going to the Queen to ask her help, he finds Don Jose pressing his suit on the indignant Queen, and kills him. The King on hearing of the Count's bravery restores to him hi a bride, and makes him Governor of Valentia. The artistes taking part were as follows :—Don Cresar de Bazan, Mr. Sidney Taylor ; Don Jose, Mr. Alex. Wood; King of Spain, Mr. Wilfred Ellison :Lazarillo. Miss Rose Keefe ; and Mari- tana, Miss Fanny De Bouffiers—a quintette who proved themselves equal to the exigencies of the occasion. The performance throughout was a complete success, the singing being a musical treat. The dresses too, were admirably selected. Mr. George Moss fulfilled the duties of pianist and conductor. The report would be incom- plete without a word of praise to the Town Clerk (Mr. J. Lyon Whittle) for the excellent arrangements made, and in these duties he was ably assisted by Mr. Stanley Wilson.

The outcry against foreign dogs raised five years ago on the introduction of the Belgian Scbipperke met with the result which usually attends protests of the kind. The good qualities of this now popu- lar little dog were indeed so extensively advertised that a Schipperke Club came shortly into existence, and this association, conjointly with the Chow Chow Club, has at length succeeded in organising a show devoted exclusively to non-British dogs. This enterprise, inaugurated on Tuesday at the Westminster Acinarium, proved to be abundantly justified, and will become an annual fixture. Chow Chows of Chinese and Japanese origin formed the staple of the exhibition. They are now established favourites. White, black, tan, and fawn colour, the breed possesses the charm of variety; and its repre- sentatives on Tuesday seemed to possess virtues of the most ingratiating description. The little Snip- perkes also came in for their share of admiration. The novel feature in the exhibition was the pre- sence of some specimens of the " Dogue de Bor- deaux," a formidable-looking brute of great fighting powers. One of the " beauties "of the show—and there were many—Was decidedly " Shalizada," an Afghan barukhzy hound, whose legs were trousered with long silky hair of the finest possible texture.

THE F,XAMINER, NTURDAY, DECEMBER 14, 1895.

A CASE OF NERVOUS PROSTRATION RESULTING FROM INDIGESTION.

They say that misery laves emnp illy, and they have had it so often it has passed into a proverb. Yet it isn't an all-round truth. Some kinds of misery detest company. They want to be left alone. They hate to be elbowed and questioned and talked to. A wounded dog will always crawl into some retired place by itself. The in- stinct of badly Injured men, after a battle is the same. Ailments that are mostly faucy, tend to set tongues waggiug. But real, genuine and dangerous diseases don't incite to speech. Crises which are big with fate usually come and go in quiet That is why Mrs. Scuffham had no desire for the social, of even her best friends at a certain time she is going to tell us about. " Up to April, Mil," she writes,'' I never knew what it was to be ill. At that time I began to feel that some- thing was amiss with me. I had no relish for my meals, and after eating, my chest felt heavy and painful, and ,ny heart would beat and thump as though it meant to leap out of its place. Presently I became so swollen round the waist that I was obliged to unloose my zlothing, as I could not bear anything to touch that part of my body. "Even the lightest food gave me pain ; a little fish setting my heart to beating at a great rate. My feet were cold, and cold, clammy sweats would break out all over me, and leaving me exhausted and worn out. At night I got no sleep to speak of, and in the morning I felt worse tired than when I went to bed. I also suffered a great deal from my feet being puffed up and score. I could scarcely get about the house. When I went shopping I had to ride to the town and back as I could only walk a few yards.

" As time went on I lost my flesh and strength more and more, and gave up hope of ever recovering the pre- cious health I had so sadly lost. I took medicines, and consulted a clever doctor at Derby who examined me and said my heart was weak. He also gave me medi. eines, but I got only temporary ease front them, and in a short time was as bad as before. All this time I was so nervous and depressed that I had no desire for company. On the contrary I seemed to want to be alone with my misery. Even a knock at the door frightened me, as though I expected bad news,yet I did not really. My nerves and fancies ran away with my knewledge and judgment. Thousands of women who have suffered in this way will understand what I mean. "Year after year I remained in this condition, and what I went through I cannot put in words, nor do I wish to try. It will answer the purpose to way that I existed thus for eleven and a half years, as much dead as alive. I spend pounds on pounds in physic, but was not a whit the better for any of it. "In October, 1892, a book was left at our house, and I read in it of oases like mine being cured by Mother Seigel's Syrup. I got a bottle from Mr. Bardel, the chemist, in Normanton Street, Derby, and when I had taken this medicine for a few days, my appetite was better and I had less pain. I kept on taking it, and soon my food agreed with me and I gained strength. "After this! never looked behind me, but steadily got stronger and stronger. When I had taken three bottles I was quite like a new woman. All the nervousness had I left me, and my heart was sound as a bell. Since than I have enjoyed good health, and all who know me say my recovery is remarkable. lam confident that Mother I Seigel's Syrup was the means, in the hands of Provi- dence, of saving my life; and out of gratitude, and in hope of doing good, I freely consent to the publication ef this statement. (Signed) (Mrs.) Ann Scuff ham, Cooper's Lane, Laceby, Grimsby, May Ist, 1885." This letter is endorsed by Mr. William J. Tollerton, of the same town, who vouches for the truth of what Mrs. Scuffham has said, as :he personally knew of the circumstances of her illness at the time they occurred No comment can add a jot to the force of this open, candid, and sincere communication. Whosoever reads it must needs be moved and convinced by it. The disease which filled this woman's life with pain and misery for nearly twelve years was indigestion or dyspep- sia, an ailment sly and cunning as a snake in the grass —and as dangerous. Send for the book of which Mn. Scuffnam speaks, and read the symptoms in order that you may know what it is, and how to deal with it. The book costs you nothing, yet it would be worth buying as if every leaf were hammered with gold.

SHIP CANAL.

LASP WEEK'S TRAFFIC.

During the seven days ending Friday, December 5, 115 sea-going vessels, with an aggregate net registered tonnage of 33,891 tons, arrived at and sailed from the various docks, wharves, &c., on the Ship Canal, as against 93 vessels of 32A88 tons during the previous week. There was a large increasa in coastwisa traffic, which was represented by 90 vessels of 18,403 tons, against 62 vessels of 11,974 tons ; while foreign traffic showed a slight diminution,the figures for last week being 25 ships of 15,488 tons,against 31 ships of 20,514 tons in prec•ding week. Twenty-one general cargoes were im- ported. 16 of stone, slate, &c., 15 of china clay, four of pig iron, two of iron pyrites, one of copperas, one of lime ash, one of pit props, one of oranges, &c., from Syria, and one of fliur, currants,and sumach. Amongst the cargoes exported were 19 of general merchandise and four of coal. The railway traffic to and from the docks is increasing rapidly, and last week was an ex- ceptionally busy one in this department, several thou- sands of tons of timber, wood pulp, iron pyrites, pig iron,cotton, fruit, and general merchandise having been despatched to inland destinations over the London and North-Western, Lancashire and 'Yorkshire, Cheshire Lines, and other railways ; while a large amount of coal, machinery, and other goods arrived at the docks by rail for shipment. During last month 20,000 tons of railway traffic were dealt with by the dock officials, nearly four times as much as was handled in the cor- ponding period of last year. It is understood that ~the Lancashire and Yorkshire Railway, via Cornbrook Junction and Ancoats, is about to begin excavating the underground branch line which will place it in direct connection with thc dock railway system. When this is completed a great stimulus will, it is anticipated, be given to the trade of the port with the surrounding towns.

NO NERVES. THE HAPPY CEHNAMAN,

An American writer, who has closely studied the sub- ject,tells us that the Chinaman is much less nervous than the E iropean. and proves his case thus. The European cannot sit still. We finger our pencil while talking. We rub our hands together, we twirl our thumbs. The Chinaman can remain any length of time in one position He will write all day like an automaton. He can stand weaving, gold-beating, or whatever may be his craft, frm morn till eve without variation of the monotony, Chi see school children are subjected to an amount of confinement, unrelieved by any change of work, which would bring Western children to the verge of insanity. The very infants in arms, instead of squirming and wriggling, as our children do, almost as soon as they are born li as impassive as their mud gods. In the item of sleep the Chinaman can sleep anywhere. None of the trifling disturbances which drive us to despair annoy him With a brick for a pillow, he can lie down ow his bed of stalks and sleep the sleep of the just. He does not want his room darkened, nor require others to be still. The cock may crow or the child cry, or the cat waul or the dog howl, without disturbing him. On public works hundreds may be seen, as the American has seen them, asleep across three wheelbarrows, with head down and mouth wide open, and a fly inside. Needleness to say, there are no hospitals for nervous diseases in China.

"IS TEE PREVENTION OF SMOKE

ECONOMICAL ?"

On Thursday last week, in the rooms of the Marine Engineers' Union, Limited, South John-street, Mr. T. Morris, F.G.S., of the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Company, Limited, Warrington, lectured to an attentive and appregiative anditory, under the presi- dency of Mr. J. A. Mills, president of the Liverpool branch. The subject of the lecture was "Is the preven- tion of smoke economical ?" In the first part of his paper he devoted himself to explaining the component parts of black smoke with the view to proving the im- possibility of consuming smoke by any known process once it is formed. Dealing historically with the various inventions designed to consume smoke before leaving the combustion chamber, he showed that, so far as was known, one and all had been futile in accomplishing the object intended, and he came to the conclusion that the prevention of smoke was not economical, the expense of rarefaction exceeding the value of the product. In pro- testing against the persecution to which coal-consuming factories and works were exposed at the hands of sani- tary inspectors, he added, as the result of about forty- five years' experience that he believed they would not have much reason to fear the nuisance inspector if they attended to a few points. Among these he stipulated upon having no air inlet except what passed through the furnace bars, an automatic feeder or an honest stoker who would feed his fuel on the grid without letting air in, the fuel to be as equal in size as possible, and the fire to be fed as lightly and frequently as practicable.—From the discussion which followed it appeared that all were not agreed with Mr. Morris on every point, but he was heartily thanked for his paper.

MR ALFRED R. SUTTON'S CONCERT.

Mr. Alfred R Sutton held his first grand con- cert at the Parr Hall, Warrington, on Friday night, and despite the boisterous weather. there was a very fair number present, Mr. Sutton had arranged an excellent pro- gramme, and the artistes who were engaged are well known to Warrington audiences, being as fol- lows : Miss Ethel Thompson, solo pianoforte ; Miss Eleanor Pickton, soprano; Miss Taylor, contralto; Mr. W. S. Howard, tenor; Mr. E. Whitehouse, baritone ; and Mr. Richard Eccles, basso. Mr Sutton's' orchestra comprising 50 performers, con- ducted by himself, also gave a number of selec- tions, the following comprising their repertoire : March, " Mendelssohn," composed by Mr. Sutton; overture," Bohemian Girl," Balfe ; " The Darkey's Dream," Lansing; symphony by Schubert in B minor (unfinished) first movement; overture, " Mirelli," GOunod ; entre-acte, " Sprites," Mr. Sut- ton; and Dances, "To Henry VIII.," German. The pieces composed by Mr. Sutton show that his talents in composition are ot no mean order,and he is to be complimented upon the excellent impres- sion they made upon the audience. Mr. E. W.Whitehouse's contributions were very successsful and were "The Bedouin's love song," and "The angel at the window." Mr. Richard Eccles and Miss Eleanor Picktou were the other soloists and their pieces were respectively "Out ou the deep,' and "Rest " (Handel). Two quartettes were given by Miss Pickton, Miss Taylor, Mr. W. S. Howard, and Mr. R. Eccles entitled " Sleep, gentle lady," and Madrigal from Sullivan's opera, "Mikado." Miss Ethel Thompson exhibited great skill and taste in the pianoforte concerto (Capriccio brillante), by Mendelssohn. The concert taken as a whole was an excellent one, and Mr. Sutton's initial venture is not without encouragement for the future.

TRAVELLING IN RURAL JAPAN.

On Monday night, :at a meeting of the Royal Geo- graphical Society, held in London, the Rev. W. Weston, read a paper descriptive of journeys made by him in the Japanese Alps. Speaking of the customs of the in- habitants of the districts through which he travelled, Mr. Weston said the Japanese in these out of-the-way mountain resorts indulge their taste for bathing to an incredible extent. In one place he knew of, where the water was just about blood heat, a man would stay in practically for a month on end, taking care, however,to place a heavy stone on his knees, to keep him from floating or turning over in his sleep. The caretaker of this particular establishment, a cheery old man of some seventy summers, himself stays in the bath the whole winter through. In the case of another spot famed ft r its thermal springs, the inhabitants apoloogised to a friend of his for what they called their dirtiness when he happened to visit their neighbourhood on a tour. "For," they said, " it is the summer time, and we are too tusy to bathe more than twice a day." " Elow,often,then," he asked," do you bathe in the winter ? " " Oh, then we have more leisure, and we can bathe four or five times a day, and the children get into the water when- ever they feel cold !" After giving some account of the flora and funa of this Alpine region, Mr. Weston des- cribed the people, whose principal characteristic was their manners. Kindly hospitality was met with where- ever one went, and good treading was not the exclusive prerogative of a particular class or the monopoly ofthe educated or the well-to-do. As illustrating the character of the people, Mr. Weston related that during a moun- taineering expedition,not previously made by a foreigner near a wild, almost uninhabited valley, the only awn- modation he could get was at a solitary yuba(bath-house) of the roughest kind. On his arrival with a request for shelter,he was received by an old gray-haired man of three score and thirteen, who combined the dignity of a prince with the simplicity of a peasant. During the two days he spent under this primitive roof he was treated with a courtesy and attention that could not be excelled. His host's only anxiety was lest he should find the acco modation too rough and rude. Without Mr. Weston wishing it, be turned out the people who were in the best room he had. He always contrived tolet his guest have the public bath to himself, and made him a present of cakes of the sulphur deposit from the solfatara hard by to take home to use in his own bath. The only fav- our ho asked was that, one evening at dinner time, he might be allowed to come upstairs and sit at the top of the ladder, in the open doorway, to see how and what the foreigner ate at dinner. When Mr. Weston was leav- ing he asked what he had to pay. How puzzled the old gentleman was ! Shakieig his head, he said he really didn't know, as he had never entertained an" honour- able Mr. Foreigner before." After much pressing to name his own price, he finally ventured to murmur, with an air almost of shame at the exorbitance of the de- mand, "Well, really, do you think 5 sen (1ld.) would be too much ? "

DIMA.GES AGAINST UNQUALIFIED MEDICAL PRACTITIONERS.

Two actions which resulted in damages against un qualified medical practitioners were tried before Judge Parry in the Manchester County Court on Monday.. In the first case judgment for £22 and costs was given against Samuel John Sherman, of lling-st.,who is adver- tised as a specialist in hernia. The plaintiff, John Howard, a mill engineer at Middleton, was induced by reading the defendant's advertisement to consult him, and he paid him first a preliminary fee of one guinea and afterwards a sum of £2l on the faith of a represen- tation that he could effect a cure. After suffering great 'pain as the result of the defendant's treatment, Ho ward consulted Mr. Walter Whitehe ad at the Royal Infirmary, and very soon obtained relief. The defendant called several persons who said they had been benefited by h treatment, but the judge held him to be guilty of fraudulent misrepresentation. The second case was of a similar character. The plaintiff, Harriet Bayliss, was aa cook in service near Bury. She consulted a man named Robert Whitehead, of Brunswick-st., who advertised himself as a specialist in certain diseas es, but whose treatment caused her unnecessary suffering, and ren- dered an operation necessary. The Judge awarded her £lO and costs.

ROBBERY AT THE BOOKSTALL AT THE CENTRAL STATION. On Monday morning it was discovered that the bookstall belonging to Messrs. W.H. Smith and Sons, at the Central Station, had been broken into. The small office in which the books in connection with the business were kept had been forcibly entered and the desk ransacked. As the manager of the stall had, however, taken the precaution on Saturday night to remove everything of appreciable value, only a few coppers were found by the thief to com- pensate him for the trouble and risk of breaking open the door. All the books were left untouched I so that it is plain that the person or persons did not intend to run any risk of discovery by having them in his possession.
