BOLD-STREET WESLEY GUILD.

LECTURE ON JOHN WESLEY.

The third lecture of the list arranged by the Bold-street Wesley Guild was given at the Bold-street Wesleyan schoolroom, on Mcnday evening, by the Rev. Dinsdale T. Young of Manchester, upon " The father of Methodism, the man himself." There was a very good attendance, and the chair was occupied by Mr. C. E. Parker, of Penketh.

After being briefly introduced by the Rev. G. D. Mason, the CHAIRMAN said that he was very pleased to be associated with the lectures held in connection with the Wesley Guild. It was a pleasure to be associated with any movement which was intended to awaken into activity the intallectual life and thought of the young people connected with the churches. (Hear, hear.) There were many ways in which they could increase their knowledge, and he did not think that they could do that better than by studying the life and character of John Wesley, who was the subject of the lecture that night, who had done so much for the raising and uplift- ing of the country. He believed that in Mr. Young, they had one who was worthy the subject, and the sub- ject was worthy of him. (Loud applause.) The Rev. DINSDALE T. YOUNG then proceeded with his lecture. He said that Lord Macauley characterised the Methodist revival—for Methodism sprang out of a re- vival—as the most remarkable moral revolution ever witnessed. In order to understand more of the charac- ter of him who providentially led that revolution, they must first of all ask what was the condition of things in this country immediately preceeding that revolution. In other words, in what kind of an England did John Wesley come. There were some people to-day who were forever croaking about "the good old times,"—(Laugh- ter)—but it would be noticed that they never gave them the date of those good old times. When were the good old times? He could not say ; but be could state emphatically that they were not the times of John Wesley. (Laughter, and hear, hear) Wesley was born at Epworth on the 17th June, 1703, during the time of Walpole, when England occupied a very low standard in morality. The chief statesmen were well known to be exceedingly immoral characters ; religion was laughed and scoffed at more especially by the " upper " circles of society ; and the nature of the drama was such as would make any person of to-day blush for shame. The habits of the masses, as well as of the classes, were deplorable in the extreme, corruption, licentiousness, and drunken- ness were familiar objects to all. So bad were the times in fact, that it had been recorded that a certain divine had said that he had only seen one Bible in the whole of Kent, and that was used to prop up a treepot. As to the ecclesiastics of the time,they were to be painted with a black,black brush. A reliable historian had said that "the decay of Dissent and of great Dissenting bodiee went hand in hand with that of the Church." Preaching the gospel was almost at an end ; the clergy were loose in their lives and lax in their doctrines. There was a general decay of religion in the minds of men. Did it not want a revolution to rid the land of these evils and short-comings ? Yes ; and thank God one did occur,the arch-revolutionist being John Wesley himself, the father of Methodism. (Applause.) Of course there were glints of light in the gloom. There was the great literary re- vival which he believed prepared the way in some degree for the great religious revival. But a literary revival was not enough either for individuals or for empires. A renaissance and a revival were not equivalent, and so, following the liter try revival came the most remarkable revolution which changed in a few years, the,whole tem- per of English society. Mr. Young then went on to deal with the father and mother ..)f John Wesley whom he eulogised in glowing language. He said that it was " Epworth and not Oxford which was the cradle of Methodism." (Applause.) After further biogtaphical details, the lecturer said that after taking holy orders, and having been advanced to priest's orders in 1728, he returned to Oxlerd in 1729, and from that time became one of the most historical persons of the English University. His changed course was soon afterwards observable, and the methodical habits of him and some of his colleagues provoked the remark from a sneering young masher of the period that " A new set of Methodists have sprung up." Method- ism then began in the University, and not amongst the low as some persons were fond of asserting. The strict habits of John Wesley and a few of his colleagues pointed to a great course of reform. He soon afterwards went out to Georgia, where he laboured two years as a foreign missionary. He had been said to be unsuccf situ! as a foreign mi-sionary.This was true,but the reason was that be went out an unconverted Ritualist. It could not be said that after he was converted from ritualism he was an unsuccessful missionary. (Applause.) The Lec- turer then proceeded to delineate in detail some of We leading characteristics of the character of John Wesley. He said he was a great scholar,a preacher of wonderful Dower aud popularity, a philosophical theologian, an historical critic, and a tremendous writer. Everyone knew he was a poet of the poets. He was also gifted with considerable humour, whilst at the same time he was one of the most composed of men. His life was one of continual industry ; he had ridden on the average every year some 5,000 miles on horseback, and that be- fore macadam was thought of. He preached two,three, or four times a day. In fact, throughout the 60 years of his ministry he preached on the average sixteen times per week. • Yet he had time for all his other work. (Applause.) His generosity was unbounded, and when he died he did not leave more than £lO besides his books his clothes, a much abused reputation, and the Method- ist Church. (Loud applause.) A vote of thanks to the Lecturer proposed by the Chairman, and seconded by the Rev. G. D. Mason,closed the proceedings.

===== 0002601_18951214_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
AN ENGLISH POLYGAMIST.

SEVEN YEARS PENAL SERVITUDE.

At the Old Bailey on Wednesday, William Henry Cadman, 54, described as a traveller, who was arrested in Manchester a short time ago on a charge of fraud, was convicted of bigamy. The prisoner's crime was really polygamy, he having gone through the ceremony of marriage with a large number of women, his object in some cases being to obtain possession ef their pro- perty. Correspondence found after his arrest showed that he had been writing to no fewer than twelve women in the capacity of husband. He now tried to villify the character of one of his victims, a proceeding which led the Recorder to pass condemnationin warm and dignified terms upon the "license of counsel." "Why," asked Sir Charles Hall of the prisoner's advocate, " try to visit the shame of their parents on the children in this way ?'' "I am instructed to ask the question," was the reply " I can well understand," retorted Sir Charles, "you being instructed to ask the question, but counsel must exercise a little discretion, and not allow themselves to be made the medium for villainous spite." The prisoner was found guilty, and the Recorder, remarking that this was the grossest case of bigamy that had ever come under his notice, passed the maximum sentence of seven years' penal servitude. The medical profession may be interested to learn that while Cadman was in Strange- ways Gaol the heartless scoundrel gave directiens that " if his end should happen his body should be disposed of to the medical people at Owens College." He was sure they would find something peculiar in respect to his brain, and—strange considerateness for his injured wife and the mother of nine of his children—the bequest would bring his family" a few ppunds."

===== 0002601_18951214_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
AN ATLANTIC LINER IN COLLISION.

The White Star steamer Germanic had just lett the Mersey on Wednesday evening for New York with 120 passengers on board, and was steaming slowly off the Crosby Lightship, when she collided during a thick fog with Messrs. Burns's Glasgow passenger steamer Corn- brae, inward bound, with 30 passengers. The Germanic crashed into the Cumbrae's forecastle, making a hole of twelve or fourteen feet. The White Star steamer stood by the Cumbrae, her bow filling the gap in the latter vessel, until all the passengers and crew had been tran- shipped. She then backed off, and awaited the founder- ing of the Cumbrae. Meanwhile, however, it was thought possible to save the ship's valuables and papers, and a volunteer crew, drawn from both ships, put cff in a boat for that purpose, but before they could reach her she sank. In the fog the boat's crew were unable to find the Germanic, and, being fog-bound, tried to hail Da ss- ing vessels. In this they were successful, the tug Gamecock ultimately taking them on board and con- veying them to Liverpool. The Germanic, which had about twenty feet of her bow plates displaced and twisted, returned to Liverpool with her own and the Cumbrae's passengers.

===== 0002601_18951214_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SHIP BUILDING DISPUTE.

It had been expected than an agreement would have been reached in the shipbuilding trade dispute by the joint conference sitting in Glasgow on Wednesday ; but the official report states that desired consummation was not reached. According to the report, the Belfast engi- neers offered to accept an immediate advance sf one shil- ling per week, with the definite promise of another simi- lar advance in April, no aFeration thereafter in wages to take place for four months. The Clyde engineers offered to accept an immediate advance of one farthing per hour in April to all in receipt of 71d. or less per hour the Belfast conditions also applying. The terms were declined by the employm's. who in return offered the Belfast men an advance of one shillitfg a week to com- mence in February and to remain undisturbed for six months,and the Clyde men an immediate advance of 1. per hour to apply to all operatives not receiving less than 7d. per hour, and a further addition in February of one farthing all round. The operatives' representatives de- clined to recommend these terms for acceptance, but agreed to submit the offers for decision by ballot of the operatives. The conference was adjourned until after the decision of the operatives had been arrived at. It is believed that the ballot will result favourably to a settle- ment.

Major Sandbach, of the Royal Artillery, who went out to Shoot " big game" in Somaliland, has died from terrible injuries received in an encounter with a lioness.

PENKETEL

ANCIENT ORDER OF' RECRABITES. —An entertainment was given in the Wesleyan Schools, Penketh, on Friday eveningweek by Bro. Past High Chief Ruler Diamond, of Southport, and a number of Rechabite friends from Warrington, Bro. Hall in the chair. There was a good attendance of members of the local lodge, Sir Wilfrid, and friends. The Chairman said that about three years ago a movement was made to establish a tent in the village, and he was pleased to say it had been brought to a successful issue. 'fhey opened the tent with seven members,and now they had 24, which showed a steady increase. He hoped they would go on increasing in numbers, and do whatever they could to advocate the principles of total abstinence. A very interesting pro- gramme of vocal and instrumental music was then gone through by Miss Ada Holt, Miss Warburton,Miss Gallop, Mr. Kilduff, Mr. Whittington, and Mr. Toddings. Bro. Diamond told a number ef amusing anecdotes relating to intemperance, and impressed upon all present to do what they could to assist in the good work of reforma- tion. Bros. Hall and Lewis also spoke. RAILWAY ACCIDENT AT FIDDLERS' FERRY.—OR Monday evening, between five and six o'clock, an accident oc- curred at the Fiddler's Ferry goods siding, on the Lon- don and North-Western Railway. A goods train wag being shunted when a wagon got off the metals and fell over on its side, fouling the main line for several hours, and doing damage to the permanent way. The traffic had to be passed through on a single line between Car- ter's House Junction, Widnes, and Sankey Bridges.

At the Leeds Ass.zes on —Monday Patrick Morley, a labourer, who was convicted of the murder of his wife at Batley on September 22 last by shooting her with a revolver, was sentenced to death. The Australian Eleven will appear at Old Trafford in the first of the three " representative " matches on June 25, end on the same famous ground against the North of England. Another match will also he played against the Lancashire Eleven. The Colonials will appear at Sheffield on Whit-Monday.

THE EXAMINER.

SATURDAY, December 14th, 1895.

WEDNESDAY was, SO far as mom- R9pudiatiln. bers of the Uovernmentj were

concerned, a day of deputations. It has been suggested with a good show of reason that it might also be described as a day of repu- diations. Lord SALISBURY when interviewed by a deputation of the • National Agricultural Union practically repudiated the idea that he had ever in the whole catalogue of his political speeches en- couraged the hopes of those interested in the im- position of protective duties on any kind of agricultural produce. Our present Prime Minister is clearly a much misunderstood man. Somehow or other Lord SALISBURY has been persistently credited, not only by his political opponents but by his political fribnds as well,with opinions which, to say the least, were not those of a convinced and consistent free-trader. His allusions to commer- cial subjects in his public speeches have nearly always been in the nature of criticisms of the results and tendencies of Free Trade, and he has again and again used a form of ex- pression in dealing with these questions which seemed to imply that if he wbre invested with the necessary power he would not be averse to a revision of the fiscal system of the country. As a result, protectionists of every description rallied to the side of the Conservatives, and they claim to have been partly in- strumental in returning the large Unionist majority at the last election. Naturally,therefore, they are now clamouring for their reward. The demand made by the deputation on Wednesday was not a large one, but if secured it would have acted admirably as " the thin end of the wedge." It was, that one shilling should be taken off the duty on beer made of British barley, malt and hops,and that the duty on foreign beer be increased by an equivalent amount. This was no doubt considered to be a very modest request by the members of the National Agricultural Union. If it had been propounded to Lord Salisbury when he was out of office, how sympathetic would have been his attitude. How he would have di- lated upon the hardships of the barley and hop growers and upon the undiscriminating zeal of the free traders. But unfortunately for tho National Agricultural Union, Lord SALISBURY is now in

office. That, it was quickly. seen, MP'COS a differ- ence. A Prime Minister with a majority of a hundred and fifty at his back can not ride off on a few sympathetic platitudes ; he is expected to em- body his sympathy in the form of effective legisla- tion. Lord SALISBURY had therefore no option but to discredit in the eyes of the old country the vote- catching devices which had been diligently em- ployed by his party prior to the General Election, He declared that he had "never held language that would induce the belief that it was to Parliament entirely or mainly that they should look for a re- volution in agriculture." Something even more pronounced Pien that was to follow. "He was sensitive," he said, "upon the suggestion that he ever promised or urged upon any audi- ence the belief that protection would return within any period to which this genera,4ion could look. Without going into the theoretic part of the question, he believed that as a matter of fact there would be no such change el' opinion within our time." It will be seen from this that LORD SALISBURY has at last, when cornered, so to speak, treated the deluded protectionists Who helped to place his Government in power to the most drastic form of cold-water cure. He has told them I plainly not only that the Government will do !nothing to promote their foolish projects, but also 1 that so far as the present generation is concerned protection is dead and done with. Protectionists are now,therefore, unless they abandoo their futile crusade, under the necessity of working for poster- ity. It is a thankless task, for even in the end posterity will judge for itf 31f, and will not, we venture to think, go with the protectionists. If the position of Lord SALISBURY was difficult I when confronted with the glmsts of past promises that of Lord GEORGE HAMILTON, the Secretary for India, was both difficult and humiliating. Lord GEORGE HAMILTON was waited upon by a very large deputation representing the whole body of the employers and operatives engaged in the Lancashire cotton trade,which made a strong pro- test against the continuance of the Indian cotton duties. The deputation in this case might have reasonably expected that their protest would be heard with willino• ears and a ready promise that it should be acted upon without delay. The pre- sent Secretary for India was, in common with a large number of other Conservatives when in op- position, strong in his denunciations of these duties. In a speech on February 21st of this year he declared that "it was necessary to protest strongly against a measure wlkich must result in the erection of a heavy hostile barrier against the importation of cotton goods into India." At that time he was of opinion that "the tax was an unfair tax as far, as Lancashire was concerned ;" that "it was dangerous to the stability of our govern- ment in India,"and that it was"a burden upon the shoulders of an already very depressed industry." At the time of the general election, when he already held the office of Secretary for India, he asserted, in a letter in aid of a Conservative can- didate in Lancashire, that his opinions were still the same as those expressed in the speech from which we have quoted. Everyone now knows the effect that the import duties question had upon the elections in Lancashire It was by far the chief cause of the Tory victories in the county. The promise had been mals about as clearly as any promise can be made by a political party, that if the Tories were returned to power the policy of their predecessors would be reversed on this particular subject. But what is the result ? The duties are still imposed, and Lord GEORGE HAMILTON, the spokesman of the government on matters pertaining to India, holds out no hope that they will be abolished. Yet the arguments against the duties are stronger now than they were at the beginning of the year when LORD GEORGE figured as one of the champions of the Lancashire cotton industry. One member of the deputation mentioned that the exports of cotton goods to India showed a decline this year of twenty-nine per cent, as compared with last year. That is an ominous fact which the whole country should take note of, for not less than twenty-five per cent, of the total exports of Great Britain consists of cotton goods ; and it was rendered all the more ominous by the further statement that " thousands of cotton operatives are walking the streets of Lancashire in a state of semi-starvation."

THE interest in the education con- The Education controversy. troversy increases rapidly as time goes on, and there are many indica- tions that it will before long be the question of the hour. Sir \Yuman HARCOURT has written a vigorous letter on the subject in which he points out the weak pine in the case for the voluntary schools as that case is preeented by some of its ladvocates. Sir WILLIAM is not surprised at nor does he regret the efforts that are being made to improve the position of the voluntary schools. But he is dead against the " levelling down process of equalising Board schools and voluntary schools. If Board Schools are more efficient than voluntary schools, as some of the Bishops say they are, Sir WILLIAM Ile.e.courirs solution of the problem is the raising of the voluntary schools to the higher level and not the degrade- ' tion of the Board schools to the lower level. That is a proposition which will, we believe, have the assent of a vast majority of the Eoglish people. Sir WILLIAM is not alarmed by the growth of the School Board rate. He is of opinion that "it would be strange and deplorable indeed if in a quarter of a century the educational standard had not been raised, and the conception oi what is required had not been enlarged." He also points out that even now the standard of our education is far below that of the United States and many Continental nations. He therefore, strongly pro- tests against any attempt "to put back the clock," and he predicts that "episcopal obscurantism ' will not prevail. In the " Nineteenth Cen- tury" of this month, Mr. LYULPH STANLEY I also deals with the " Re-opening of the Education Settlement," and he gives some figures to show that the voluntary schools have not lost ground since the" new departure" of 1870. "We find,"he says, "that in 1869 the subscriptions were 78.30. a head—they ard now Os. 6id. a head ; that in 1869 the grants were 9s. 7d, a head—they are now 18s. 11d. a head (besides Science and Art grants of probably 4d. a head) ; that the income from tees and fee grant is 12s. 11d., as against Bs. 4d. in 1869, and the number of scholars in voluntary schools is 2,416,327 in average attend- ance, against 1,062,999 in 1869." It will be seen from all this that as the controversy increases in warmth, the Bishops are not having it all their own way.

During the gale on Friday atternoon, Mrs. Mills, of Rutland-st., received a severe scalp wound. It appears that she was crossing the street near to the Waterloo hotel when two conveyances were coming in opposite directions, and she was struck by the shaft of one of them on the head.receiving a nasty gash. Dr.McGennan was speedily called to attend Mrs. Mills, and she is now progressing favourably. SALT SHIPMENTS FROM RUNCORN AND WESTON.

There has been an appreciable increase in the ship- ments of both white and rock salt during November from Runcorn and the Ship Canal, and Weston Point. From the former place the total amount shipped was 3,079 tons as compared with 2,375 tons in the corres- ponding month last year, and trom Weston Point th,. amount shipped was 1,470 tons as compared with 908 tons.

RUNCORN MUSICAL SOCIETY.

On Monday evening the members of the above-men- tioned society will give their second concert of the season in the Public Hall, Runcorn. The first part Of the pro- gramme will consist of the performance of Barnett's "Ancient Mariner." The music was composed expressly for the Birmingham Triennial Musical Festival, 1867, since when it has taken its place amongst the classics of English music. It is a piece abounding in delightful passages and will give plenty of scope to both chorus and soloists. Some miscellaneous pieces will comprise the second part. The special artistes engaged are Miss Mary Langdon, Mrs. Burstall, Mr. William Green, and Mr. Arthur Barlow. and there is every promise of a really first-class concert being given.

.. . GREEN WAY-ROAD BOARD SCHOOL CONCERT.

The annual concert by the e-cl;lar; ait-endine Green- way-road Board School will be given on Wednesday and Friday neat. Many will remember the success of the last concert when the operetta "Mr. Nobody" was performed, and it is anticipated that this year's concert will even surpass last year's performances. The objects which are to benefit by the proceeds will commend themselves to the general public. A portion will be devoted to the orphan fund of the National Union of Teachers, and the remainder to the scholars' prizs fund, an institution which has greatly encouraged the children to attend regularly at school. As is well-known, the schools stand in the front rank, as evidenced by the Inspector's reports. Mr. S. Taylor, J.P., C.C., will preside on Wednesday evening, and Mrs. Maitland Wood will distribute the prizes. On Friday evening, Mr. Wilson Blackledge, the well-known humorist will give a number of character sketches, when the children will repeat the performance of the operetta "Pay of the Pied Piper," and give in addition several miscellaneous items.

THE NEW SHIP CANAL BILL. , The General Purposes and Parliamentary Committee had under consideration at their meeting on Tuesday evening, the proposals contained in the Ship Canal Bill, to be submitted during the forthcoming session of Par- liament. Mr. P. Speakman, J.P., C.C., was in the chair. As a copy of the bill had not been received by, the Coun- cil, it was decided to wait until it was forwarded before taking any action. In the meantime the Medical Officer of Health and the Surveyor were requested to report as to the effect the closing of tke sluices would be likely to have on the town. The opinion was expressed that it was necessary for the Council to do all within its power to preserve the health of the town. EXPERIENCES OF A RUNCORN CREW IN THE GALE. During the gale of last week' the crew of the steamer Thursby, in charge of Captain John Looby, and which frequently visits Runcorn, had very trying experiences. It appears that it set sail from Yarmouth on the s:b inst., but owing to the hurricane which prevailed the ' vessel was driven at least 200 miles out of its course,and received serious damages. They were fortunate, how- ever, in getting into port,after being buffeted about for two days in a most tempestous gale. THE PROPOSED NEW CHEMICAL WORKS AT WESTON.

READING ROOM AND RECREATION HALL FOR WESTON.

THE OPENING CEREMONY.

The formal opening of the reading room and recreation hall which has been fitted up at Weston took place on Wednesday evening. Mrs. Maitland Wood performed the opening ceremony in the presence of a large com- pany. The room has been built during the past year by the men and young men connected with the Weston church from toe materials of a block of wooden houses bought by the Rev. W. H. Stables from the Manchester Ship Canal Company. It is nicely situated just behind the playground of the school, on laud lent by Major Orred. It is 45ft. long and 18ft. wide, and is papered and varnished throughout. 13y means of a moveable partition a small room can be cut off, and it is intended ta use this portion for reading purposes only. The large room which is supplied with draughts, chess, dominoes, ltc., is intended to be used as a recreation room. The building ssill seat 150 people very comfortably. At the opening ceremony, the Rev. W. H. Stables who conducted the proceedings, apologised for the absence of the Vicar, the Rev. Canon Maitland-Wood, whodie was wry to say, was not much better. He then gave a short history of the movement which had led to the acquirement of the room, after which, Mrs. Wood de- clared the room open.

I'OREIGN MISSIONAIY ANNIVERS,kRY

AT CAMDEN.

In Camden Wesleyan Chapel, Lowlands-road, on Sun- day, foreign missionary services were held. In the afternoon there was a large congregation, when " Sister Annie "delivered an address on "Our Father." It was listened to with great attention, and the manner in which the lady dealt with the subject was much appre- ciated. The preacher in the evening was the Rev. T. Brackenbury. On each occasion the choir, under the conductorship of Mr. S. Bazley, sang anthems. Collec- tions were taken on behalf of the Wesleyan Foreign Mis- sionary Society. The annual missionary meeting was held on Tuesday evening when there was a crowded attendance. The chair was occupied by Mr. P. Speakman. J. P., C.C., and he was supported by the Rev. W. George, Messrs. Harrison, S. Beckett, J. Rawlinson, Edward Taylor, and others. In the course of his remarks, the Chairman referred to his visit to Palestine, and spoke of the thoroughness with which the missionaries did their work as seen by himself. He also spoke of the undoubted success of missionary effort. It the church must suc- ceed they must take an intelligent interest in the work of foreign missions. In consequence of the chairman hav- ing to leave for another meeting, the chair was later on, taken by Mr. Beckett, who complimented the missionary workers at Camden on the success which had attended their efforts. The missionary report was read by Mr. Edward Taylor, the circuit lay secretary, and showed that ,the sum of £279 4s. 2d. was raised in the Runcern circuit for the Wesleyan Foreign Missionary Sociely. The amount raised at Camden was as follows: Public meeting and sermons £9 15s. 11d., collection by Miss Temperley £2 84. 6.1.. Sunday school collections £2O Ils. 9d.,total £32 154. 23. —The Rev. A. W. Hall,for some years missionary on the Gold Coast,then delivered an address. It was unusually interesting because of the information it contained:regarding the present expendi- tion to Ashanti. It was evident that the rev, gentle- man knew well the cout try through which the English troops will have to traverse in order to reach Coomassie, and deal with the Ashanti King. He gave a most graphic account of the habits of the people , showing their plain need of Christianity and civilisation. He exhibited a large number of very interesting curios gathered by him durii'ps his sojourn in Western 1 Africa. He earnestly appealed for the continued gener- ous support of the Camden friends to the work of the missionary society.—Mr. Robinson gave a series of' de. lightlul limelight pictures, illustrative of life in Ceylon. The descriptive readings were delivered by the Rev. W. George. The collections on Sunday and Monday amounted to nearly £7.—The Rev. Vs. George acknow- ledged the services of the gentlemen who had helped to make the meeting a Success, and the proceedings then terminated.

NEWTON URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL.

A meeting of this Board was held at the Town Hall, Earlestown, on Monday, the chair being occupied by Mr. C. B. F. Borron, J.P., C.C., and the following members were also present, Messrs. W. Worsley (law clerk).

THE PROCEEDINGS OF THE LAST MEETING,

Before the business of the meeting was proceeded with, the Chairman said that he wished to express his regret at their being called together.fhat day. He trusted and hoped sincerely that no similiar occurrence would again happen in that Cauncil to that which took place the other day. (Hear, bear.) For himself, perso:.al abuse, and personal insults weighed but lightly, but as they had placed him to the chair, the dignity of the chair he was determined to maintain. (Hear, hear.) To promote the business of the Council, be would, when the chair was assailed ask the Council to adjourn day by day to ten o'clock the following morning so that the work of the district should be ca.ried out. With those remarks they would proceed to business. They now stood adjourned to the Highway Committee's minutes which bad been proposed and seconded. Mr. Beatty said that before the minutes of the High- ways Committee were passed, he would like to say that he had not voted for the censure of Mr. MacMahon last week. He had not voted at all. The Clerk : The minutes do not state that Mr. Beatty voted for the censure, and, therefore,there is no mistake as far as the minutes of the Council are concerned. Mr. Gilmour : Our LAW Clerk only asked for the " Ayes," and took it for granted that all had voted for " Ayes." Mr. McMahon asked for the minutes to be read but the Chairman said that unless Mr. McMahon or Mr. Gil- mour were going t 3 express their regret for the proceed- ings of the last meeting, the Council must preceed with the minutes of the Highways Committee. Mr. Gilmour said that he did not wish to say whether he would apologise, hut he wished to know how it was that Mr. Slee was spoil the sub-commitee about which so much was said at the last meeting. The Chairman ; You are out of order. Mr. Gilmour : But I want to know about that sub- committee. _ .

.... The Chail man : I call you to order. Mr. Gilmonr : Very well, I will obey your ruling. The minutes of the Highways Committee were then put and casSed. OAS AND WATER COMMITTEE. .

The minutes of this committee have already ap- peared in the "Examiner." The confirmation was moved by Mr. Smith seconded by Mr. Simpson.—Mr. Gilmour wished to know why the incandescent lamp in High-sr. had not been lighted.—Mr.Smith replied that he was 120: aware that it had not been lighted, but he would inquire about R.—Mr. Emmett said that according to an order made, four lamps should he placed in Regent-st., and he wished to know why two had only been erected—Mr. Smith was unable to answer that question in the absence of the gas manager, but he would answer it at the next meeting of the committee.—The minutes were then car- ried unanimously. KAYE HAS A GRIEVANCE.

The Rev. E. W. W. Kaye, in moving the adoption of the minutes of this committee which have already ap- peared in our columns, aeed lt whether a certain state- ment of accounts mentioned in them had been pre- sented to the Council, and if PO by whom ? He also wished to know whether a certain grant received from the County Council had been paid over to the Viaduct Technical Instruction Committee. Mr. Ormand (interrupting) : This is a question for the committee and net for the Council. We have to confirm or otherwise thee minutes. Mr. Kaye is asking ques- tions quite outside the minutes. The Chairman : I do not deprive any gentleman the privilege of asking questions. I must rule that Mr. Kaye is in order in asking these questions. Mr. Kaye (proceeding) said that he believed that the grant alluded to had been paid over to the Viaduct Tech- nical Instruction Committee. If that had been done he would like to know by whose authority it had been done. Some time ago it had been agreed that no money should he paid before being sanctioned by the Council. He had been Chairman of the Technical Instruction Committee for about six months, and he was sorry to say that he , had been kept almost entirely in the dark with reference ' to the financial portion of the committee's business. He did not know whether it had been done purposely or not. He had not had a chance of opening, or knowing I the contents, of certain communications, whilst ether members of the Council had had a knowledge of the con- tents. This was, in his opinion, a rather irregular proce- . durieand he thoueht the Council would bear him out in saying that no communication should be opened and I looked at by any member of the Council without being seen first by the Chairman. Mr. Thompson seconded tho minutes. Mr. Ormand : May I ask Mr. Kayo to name the gentleinan who has opened the letters before he has had sight of them. The Chairman said that fortunately, they had the Secretary of the Viaduct Technical InstructioniCorn- mittee amongst them. If he was agreeable to make an explanatioe he (the chairman) would like to hear them, and he would, therefore, ask Mr. Ormand to kindly ex- plain. Mr. Ormand thought Mr. Have had not been fair in raising these questions without first giving proper notice of them. Mr. Smith had just been allowed to defer his answer until the committee met, and the present case was very similar to that of Mr. Smith, Now the Chair- man would establish a precedent if he called upon him to answer questions which he (Mr. Ormand) contended were purely committee matters. Mr. Kaye might think it was clever,and that he was doing a good thing by sprireeing this mine upon them that day; but he (Mr. Ormand) did not think it v, as at all gentlemanly, to say the least, to bring up such matters then without notice. He should, therefore, object to give any explanation whatever except in committee, which was the proper place for such explanations. Mr. MacMahon stated that be thought a chairman ought to know something about his own committee and not come to the Board about it. If he was chairman of a comm.ttee he would make himself acquainted with the business of the Committee, or give it up. (Mr. Ormand : Hear, hear.) Mr. Gilmour said th.at he never in his experience heard such questions asked by a chairman of committee. He ablini red and destested such irregularities and the chairman of the Board acquiesing on it. The Chairman : I call you to order. Mr. Emmett said that like Mr. Gilmour, he thought it unfair of the chairman of the Technical Instruction Com- mittee to raise the questions in Council. If he wanted info, elation surely there were two or three members from whom he could procure it, instead of boring the Council with such matters. He (Mr. Emmett) was not at all surprised at the Council having to break up in the way they had done last Monday if committees did not know their business. It was such questions as Mr. Kaye had raised that caused the Council to get into such dis- grace. Mr. The mpson complained, as a member of the com- mittee, of being utterly in the dark as to the two ques- tions raised by Mr. Kaye. He had heard no more than , the Council had as to what had become of the letter,and had not heard anything of it since it was first brought up. With regard to the payment of the money he was utterly ignorant, and was surprised when he heard it had been paid. He should like to know also whether it had been paid, and by whom ? Mr. Emmett rose to a point of order, saying that Mr. Thompson was asking the same question as Mr. Kaye. The Chairman said that Mr. 7 hompson was perfectly in order. He was speaking on the questions raised. Mr. Thompson, proceeding, said it was satisfactory to know that the money had come, but it was hardly In accordance with the present system of carrying on the Council's business to allow money to be handed over to a committee without their approval. With regard to what he considered the strong remarks against the chairman of the committee, he might say that he too (Mr. Thomp- son) had not been long a member of the committee, and was not supposed to know very much about its work. (Laughter, and cries of "You don't.'') However, his friends aeross the table might laugh, he supposed that he knew quite as much as they as to how the business should be conducted. (Hear, hear, and Mr. Gilmour: " Question.") He did not think that anything was lest by keeping one's temper, so he would advise certain of the members not to get excited. (Laughter.) Mr. MacMahon remarked that this was a debateable , matter, and Mr. Kaye had no right to introduce it at that meeting. Mr. Ormand also objected, and wished to know from the Law Clerk whether Mr. Thompson was in order. Mr. Thompson was speaking upon matters which were not within the scope of the Council, but which were for the committee to deal with. Mr. Thompson said he knew nothing whatever of the letters mentioned. He must be troubled with a very short memory. He asked the Law Clerk to say whether, while Mr. Thompson was at the meeting, the letters were not read,and whether he (Mr. Ormand) did not make an explanation of them. It passed his comprehension why he raised questions about these things that day. The Law Clerk ruled Mr. Thompson in order, and in replying to the second question of Mr. Ormand said : The letters were read,ancl Mr.Ormand did make the ex- planation he said he did. The Rev. E. W. W. Kaye replied, but was on more than one occasion interrupted. . _ -

The Chairman observed he was sorry Mr.Ormand had not thought proper to publicly state clearly and plainly what had taken place with the accounts and money. He had sheltered himself by saying that it was a matter for the committee, and he would have to take the rezpen- sibility upon himself. Mr. Ormand : lam quite willing, sir, to take the responsibility. The minutes were then put and carried. TEE APPOINTMENT OF ASSISTANT OVERSEER. The Chairman announced that the Local Government Board had acceded to the request of the Council by giv- ing. them authority to appoint assistant overseers for the district. The meeting then closed.

TAKING COLD.

A " Cold " is not necessarily the result of a high or low temperature. A person may go from a hot bath directly into a cold one, or :oto snow even, and not take cold. He may remain out in the coldest atmosphere until chilled through, and still not take cold. On the contrary,he may take cola by pouring a couple of table- spoonfuls of water upon some parts of his dress, or by standing in a door, or before a stove, or by sitting near a window or other opening, where one part of the body is colder than another part. Let it be kept in mind that uniformity of temperature over the whole body is the great thine to be looked after. It is the unequal heat upon different parts of the body that produces Colds, by disturbing the uniform circulation of the blood, which in turn induces congestion of some part. Keep the feet always dry and warm, avoid draughts of air, keep the system braced up by plenty of sleep and good food, and by a powerful Tonic, and you will be proof against a Cold and its results. The very best Medicine, either for reventing or curing a Cold, is

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

ANXIOUS TO SEE THE NEW CHIEF CONSTABLE. Jane Johnson, 9, Silver-square, a well-known char- acter at the Warrington Police Court, was charged on Monday, before the Mayor and Alderman Harrison,with being drunk and disorderly in Silver-square, on Decem- ber 4. The defendant who pleaded guilty said that she had been at a christening and that she and her husband had had a drop of drink. Her husband locked her out and told he that he would not let her in until she had seen the new chief at the police station. She was seen by a policeman and suinvnoned. She was glad to see the new chief and hoped he would not be hard on her. (Laughter.)--Mr. Talbot : She has had too many looks at the old chief I think. (Laughter.) Mr. Talbot here- upon handed in the character sheet of the defendant which was a rather formidable one, and the magis- trates fined her 53. and costs or seven days'. Defend- ar'; .".id that she would pay at the other end.

SCANDALOUS TREATMENT oa. A Camp. William Wilkinson and Eden Mercer were charged at the Warrington Borough Court on Thursday with wil- fully neglecting a child Mary Ellen, on the precious night. Inspector McAteer said that the child was six weeks old. The two defendants tramped the country from one place to another. They had been to Wigan, St. Helens, Golborne, and Manchester, and arrived in Warrington ten days ago. They went to the lodging house of Jeremiah Shorrcoks. Be was informed ot the condition of the child on the previous night, and the woman was drunk when he visited her. He examined L.l-,e child, and found it to be thoroughly emaciated, the bones protruding through the skin. In fact, it was al- most like a hying skeleton. It was indeed in such a condition that it could hardly recover.—Jeremiah Shor- rocks, lodging tious, keeper, Ship Yard, said that the two prisoners had lodged at his house for ten days. On Wednesday night he heard a row at the next door, and on going there he found the two prisoners quarrel- ling and the female prisoner was hitting the male pri- soner with the child. She also hit witness with it, and at length threw it at Wilkinson.—Similar evidence was given by Mrs. llorrocks and Mrs. Butler,—Inspector McAteer said that he would withdraw the charge against Wilkinson in order that he could call him as a witness. On Wilkinson going into the box he said that he had lived with the female prisoner for three weeks, though he had known her for some considerable time. She was rather hot tempered, but she had not thrown the child deliberately at him the previous night. The prisoner belonged to St. Helens.—X.S. Smith said that when he was called in the previous night to see the female pri- soner she was behaving in a disgraceful manner, as' she was under the influence of drink. She also behaved in , a similar fashion in the cells during the night, and she pressed a jug against the child so hard that he had to break it to get it away.—Toe prisoner was sent to gaol for one month.

Witness said he did not know, whereupon prisoner said, "It is worth 225, I do not want much for it, and I will take 55." Prisoner subsequently asked witness for 4s. for it. Witness told him to call again, and the prisoner left the shop. The police were then Inforn2ed of the matter.— P C. Thomas Barnett stated that he went to the Golden Grove Inn, Lyme-st., and found prisoner in the bar. Witness asked him where the ring was, and he then produced it. Witness took him into custody, and on the way to the Police Station prisoner said "I bought it at Booth's ironmonger's shop in Wigan yesterday for ail" When charged with the offence prisoner replied "I was doing nothing when you c .me to me."—lhe Chief Constable informed the Bench that the offence oltts known as the " ring-dropping " trick, and the prisoner had tried to make Mr. Green think that he was getting A good bargain. The ring was only a common one. and worth about one penny.—Prisoner was sent to gaol for one month with hard labour. THE ROBBERY BY A WARRINGTON

CASHIER.

! Thomas Moore, late cashiet in the employ of Messrs. Robert Garnett and Sons, Sankey-streer, cabinet makers, was again brought before the Warrington magistrates, on Saturday, charged with having on the 29h Novem- ber stolen a bag containing £4O in gold, the property of his employers. The Head Constable (Mr. Luke Talbot) said the charge against prisoner was that of stealing £4O in cash. The prisoner had been in the employ of the firm for about five months as clerk, and latterly he had had charge of the ledger. Not until recently, however, had he had charge of the cash. One of his duties was to send to the bank ' for moneys for the payment of wages. Between eleven and twelve o'clock, the prisoner handed the firm's cheque to Mr. P. C. Garnett for the suns of £7O, and that young gentleman went to Parr's Bank to draw the money, atter which he returned to Sankey-street, , bringing with him £6O in gold and £lO in I silver, He counted the money in the presence of the prisoner. The prisoner also counted the money, and it was then placed in bags on the cashier's desk. After that the prisoner remarked to Mr. Garnett, jun., that he had better not leave the office until he had seen Mr. Robert Garnett. When the , prisoner returned Mr. Garnett, jun., asked him if he re- quirel him further, and the reply was " No." Young Mr. Garnett, then left the office, the prisoner being left alone with the cash. Several sums had been deducted for various purposes from the £7O brought from the bank, and £4O in gold and £lO in silver were placed in canvas bags. The prisoner was seen to leave the premises at five minutes past one ; and Mr. Philip Garnett knowing that Moore had absented himself two or three days without leave, and suspecting he was going away to drink, he followed him. He went in a roundabout way in the direction of Latchford. Part of the way the prisoner rode in a cart, the driver of which he had bailed, and part of the way he walked. Young Mr. Garnett returned to Warrington, and informed his father of the circumstances of the case. Then the police were communicated with, and the prisoner was arrested in he Bear's Paw, Frodsham, the same evening, and brought to Warrington by Detective-Sergeant Monaghan. In his possession was found upwards of £36, and £1 had been traced to the driver of a trap which prisoner bad given him as a gratuity. The prisoner was charged with the robbery, and he admitted that he had taken the money, but was sorry, and willing to reim- burse it.

The Chairman remarked that,no evideace,was needed, as the off,mce was admitted. .

Mr. R. Garnett said he had found the prisoner to be a competent man, but had only given him the position of cashier temporarily. His conduct, except with regard to the present offence, had been good, and he had not found any other misappropriations. He (Mr. Garnett) was inclined to think that the man was under the influ- ence of drink when he took the money. He, therefore hoped the Bench would deal with prisoner under the "First Offenders' Act."

Prisoner, who felt his position very acutely, said that he was sorry for what ha had done. He had been un- well, and had taken some stimulants, which had made him unconscious of what he was doing. A dishonest motive had never entered his head. He hoped the Bench would not send him to prison, or his life would be ruined. He had been separated from his wife and family, and all his friends, and it was very hard to bear. After a lengthy discussion the Chairman stated that having given careful consideration to the remarks of Mr. Garnett, they had taken as lenient a view of the case as possible, and the judgment of the Court was that prisoner should go to gaol for two months with hard labour.

HALTON.

PAROCHIAL CommirrEE.—A meeting of this Commit- tee was held on Saturday afternoon' when the chair was occupied by the Rev. G. D. Wray. There were also pre- sent :—Messrs. Thomas Wright, Jeacock, Looker,Acker- ley, Barrow, and J. Farrington (inspector of nuisances), and Mr. J. Ashton (clerk). The Clerk read a letter from the Loral Government Board with reference to the pollution of Halton Brook, and it was resolved on the motion of Mr. Jeacock, seconded by Mr. Ackerley, that it be again referred to the Medical Officer of Health for him to consider and advice what ought to be done with reference to Halton Brook and the alleged pollution thereof by the flow of sewage, with the view to meeting the recrtirements of the Local Government Board, and that he he requested to ask the County Medical Officer to inspect the brook and point out to him what he sug- gested shonld be done, and if he agreed to report to that effect. With regard to the cases of scarlet fever, it was resolved that inasmuch as parents would not allow their children to attend school owing to its prevalence in the township, the Medical officer of Health be requested to again consider the question of the closing of the schools, and to recommend that they be closed until the 6th of January next.—These minutes were confirmed at the minutes or the Runcorn Rural Council on Tuesday, on the motion of Mr. Wright, seconded by Mr. Stokes.

The population of the world averageg one hundred- and nine women to every one hundred men. Eight- ninths of the sodden deaths are those of males.

The larynx of a man in Sydney. Australia, became useless, through disease, and he lost his voice. Prof. Stuart, of the University of Sydney, made an artificial one, and it can be so regulated as to make the voice soprano, tenor, contralto, or bass, at will.

In the Salford Hundred Court of Record on Tuesday a young woman named Buckley obtained a verdict, with £5O damages, in an action for breach of promise of marriage against George Henry Matti, a pork butcher. The parties live at Harpurhey.

RUGBY FOOTBALL. THE NORTHERN UNION. WARRINGTON v. MANNINGHAM.

The first fixture between the above mentioned teams was played off on Saturday at Manningham. The kick off was timed from 2.45 p.m., and consequently a very early start had to be made from Warrington, the team leaving at ten o'clock. The weather appeared to te some- what favourable at that time, but when Manchester was reached a snowstorm came on,and from that point up to Yo,rkshire, it came down in thick flakes. When the border was crossed however, the snow stopped falling, and the sun shone out, which led the players to believe that after all, they would not have a bad day. On reaching the Manningham ground which rejoices in the name of Valley Parade, these hopes were very rudely dashed to the ground. A large number of men had ben employed to cart off the snow, with the result that when this operation was completed, the ground resembled a swamp. Thera was scarcely a blade of grass to be seen, and had the game been played on a ploughed field, the conditions would not have been much worse. One spot in particular, and this was where the players operated most frequently was a veritable quag- mire. Needless to say,the state at the ground completely spoiled any open play, and the game was a scramble from beginning to end. The plights into which tho players fell, and the sights they exhibited when they had been " grounded " caused endless delight to the specta- tors who were most good humored throughout. War- rington were minus Barber, Broady, Nevins, and Swift, their places being occupied by O'Brien, Carey, A. N. Other, and Fairhurst. A. N. Other proved to be Clark and he for the first time this season played half back for the first team. Manninghain were also without the ser- vice of Lorimer their full back, Sunderland one of their halves, and Clegg a forward, but the plsyers in their places played a very good game, Brown being especially safe at full back. There were about 2,000 spectators present during the course of the match, and the follow- ing were the players on either side : Warrington : Heesom, full back ; Burton, Cross, O'Brien,and Carey, three quarters; floughton and Clark, half backs ; Burrows, Andrews, Barrett, Hill, Taylor, Wilcock, and Fairhurst, forwards. Manningham Brown, full back ; Tolson, Pickles, Atkinson and Needham, three quarters ; Newton and Williamson, half backs; Barraclough, Wilson, Wilkinson, Bamford, Bradley, Froctor, Whiteoak and Padgett, forwards.

Barraclough kicked off and in response, Houghton and Hill dribbled to the centre where Pickles securing sent in touch. From the line out Manningham made consider- able progress but were penalised for off-side play. Cross took the kick but Brown's response more than counter- balanced the advan'age gained thereby. A free kick now fell to Newton which put Manningham on the offensive, and a flying kick came in the direction of Heesom. The latter managed to secure it but being hard pressed by the opposing forwards, he made a mis- kick, and the ball went over the Warrington goal line. The scow which was pretty thick at this point, pre- vented the ball from going over the dead tall line before Whiteoak touched it down, and thus a very lucky try fell to the Valles, Paraders after only two minutes play. Proctor failed to kick a goal. From the kick out by Cross, the Manningham forwards came down the field in a body with the ball, and when the quarter flag was reached, Wilkinson must have thought he was in his own quarters, for he gave the ball a tremendous kick which, of course, Rent it dead. Cross again kicked out, and Clark met Brown's return by a fine dribble to centre but he found Pickles a stumbling block, and the latter sent the ball in touch near the 25 flag. O'Brien secured immediately afterwards, but his kick was returned with interest by Brown, but by a sudden effort, Warrington at length got to the opposite end of the field chiefly by the exertions of Burton, Houghton and Barrett. The finest play of the afternoon, however, again gave Man- ningham the advantage for Newton securing from a scrimmage passed to Williamson, he to Pickles, and afterwards to Atkinson and Needham who passed and repassed, and the latter appeared to have the line at his mercy, when Heesom pluckily rushed up, and brought him to the ground. The movement was deservedly applauded, and Heesom salso deserved the highest credit for this frustrat- ing what seemed a certain score. An injudicious kick by one of the Warrington forwards enabled White- oak to make his mark in a favourable position, but Barraclough failed to kick a goal. Pickles again made his mark after the drop out in a still more favourable position, but Barraclough was again unable to put the oall over the crossbar. Soon after the drop out,Newton made a grand run right through the Warrington team. but fortunately Burton was able to push him into touch before he was actually dangerous. Tlais was followed by a fine dribble by Hill, Burrows and Tayler,and Brown only just prevented Warrington from scoring by the merest shade. From now to half time play was very even, and Manningham crossed over with the score of one try to nil in their favour.

Hill restarted the game and exchange kicking between Brown, Houghton, Needham, Heesom, Brown, and Cross ended in favour of the homesters. Barrett tackled Newton immediately afterwards just as he was getting off, and fleesom also distinguished himself by a kick into touch while surrounded by opponents, and War- rington began to attack to some purpose. Things looked promising for Warrington when a passing movement initiated by Clark and taken part in by Cross and Carey took place, but the latter was unable to pass Tolson. From the ensuing scrimmage, Barrett gained consider- able ground by a kick and follow up by means of Which he tackled Atkinson before he could get his kick in. After a prolonged visit to the Matsningham lines, the P4rader woke up, and by means chiefly of Newton and Needham, they once more crossed the centre line but Houghton was a little too alert and tackled well. Another mark fell to Tolson who tried a drop at goal but this was a failure and only resulted in a minor. The ball was sent over the line after the drop out and Cross touched down, but by some means or other the ball went out of Cross's possession and Williamson dashing up gained a try. The referee, however, rightly ruled against him,and only a minor was allowed. Warring- ton again had a look in by means of passes between Houghton, Cross, Burton. and O'Brien but the latter spoiled the sequence by failing to bold the ball and a good chance was lost. Manningham again came to the fore, and Clark just touched down, in time. From the drop out Warrington again attacked and Thornton had hard lines from following up the kick, and a moment later, Burrows, Hill, and Houghton, indu'ged in a passing movement in the home three quarters,and had anyone been to hand to receive Hough- ton's pass a score would certainly have ensued./is it was, he was tackled from behind only a short distance from the line. From now to the close the game was slightly to the advantage of Warrington, but neither side had any more chances of scoring, and thus Warrington lost by a try scored two minutes from the commencement of the game. Heesom was very safe with the exception of his one mistake, and by his plucky play he averted danger on frequent occasions. Burton and Cross were the best of the threequarters, though the wing players had not much to do. Clark and Houghton played well together, while the famous Manningham forwards were not very far ahead of Warrington. Taken as a whole the game was very even, and the result to have been in ac- cordance with the game, ought to have been a pointless draw. RUNCORN v. BRIGHOUSE RANGERS.

WIDNES v. BRADFORD.

At Widnes on Saturday in boisterous weather. Widnes had their full strength but Cooper and Dobson were short from the visitors' team. William Sugden started for Bradford, who went straight away towards Widnes end, Golding returning the compliment with a clean I punt. There was a quick run immediately made to Smith's charge, that player being almost beaten, Booth kicking outside. Johnson collared his man, and Drum- ' mond receiving initiated a good run, but progress was barred a few yards from Bradford's line, Bairstow com- ing to the rescue. For a brief interval the venue was changed. Golding kept the ball too long, thus giving the Yorkshiremen an opportunity, which, however, Brennan promptly neutralised. Widnes proved capable at defence, and after a few dangerous rushes had been effectually dwelt with Drummond got the leather away, H. Peters earning a round of applause for a smart, though fruitless, run. Passing by the Bradford backs recovered for a moment the lost ground, but a return was badly taken by one of the visitors. The game was hotly contested in Bradford quarters, and on two conse- cutive occasions Smith and Crompton both kicked into touch. A free kick to Widnes missed scoring by the merest chance, the ball hanging in the air against the goal, and finally falling right under the posts. This was distinctly hard lines, and shortly afterwards H. Peters experienced hard luck in failing to get over. Half time —Widnes nil, Bradford nil. The second half play was in favour of the visitors. Hail descended heavily, and several flashes of lightning did not add comfort to the proceedings. Widnes defended sternly, but eventually Walter Sugden got through, Bairstow failing at goal. Cheered by this success Bradford renewed the attack, and a muddle by Golding almost let them in, Murphy and Nevitt coming to the rescue. Bradford got through again, Bairstow converting. They also secured a dropped goal, and won by one goal, one dropped goal, one try, twelve points, to nil.

LANCASHIRE v. YORKSHIRE

The first meeting of representatives of these counties, under the auspices of the above union, took place at Oldham on Saturday, before 10,030 spectators. The ground was covered with snow, overwise the arrange- ments were most complete. There was no alteration in the Lancashire team, and only one in the Yorkshire ranks. Varley started on behalf of Lancashire, and Lor- rimer returned finely to touch. Lees came away from touch, but Lorimer returned, Barber again assisting in carrying play into Yorkshire territory. Wood and Rigg were now conspicuous, and from the latter's kick Varley punted to Lorrimer, who returned finely, and Hurst failing to gather the ball, a short kick landed t e ball into Winstanley's hands, the player standing on the line. His return was charged by Hughes, and the latter bad no difficulty in obtaining Yorkshire's first try, Lorrimer falling at goal, On resuming, Varley checked an aggres- sive Yorkshire movement, but Firth replied with inter- est, and scrimmaging of a desperate character ensued. A pass by Rigg to Wood gave the latter a chance to score, but he lost the ball when a yard from the line. Bonsor came away with a rush, and Lorri- mer luckily replying, Winstanley was again at fault, and the Lancashire line was placed in j ,ipardy. Long kicking gave the Tykes an advantage, and Winstanley was grassed near the line. From the scrimmage Rigg dashed over, and though tackled grounded the ball, Sharpe improving the try. Lancashire then rushed away and forced a minor, following which Boothroyd rounded the backs, only to be brought down by Win- stanley in grand style. Firth then contributed a beau- tiful run, and the smart work of the Yorkshire halves, coupled with the fine display of their forwards, com- pletely outplayed their, opponents. After a spell of defence the Lancashire fotwards burst away, Unsworth dribbling to midfield, but half-time arrived with York- shire leading by eight points to nil. Rigg resumed after the interval, and Winstanley being again at fault Booth- royd was given a chance, and was only tackled a few inches from the line. The Yorkshireman was injured, and Dunkin took his place. A fine movement between Chapman and Lees landed the ball to Hurst, who was, however, run into touch by Rigg. A mark gave Firth a chance for gaol, but Chapman relieved, and Lancashire pressed until Wood changed the venue to half-way. Yorkshire's superiority, however, soon asserted itself, and Rigg made a dash for the home goal, but Chapman managed to kick the ball dead. Passing between Chapman, Lees, and Barber gave the latter a chance, and the Warrington quarter almost landed a goal. Chapman again missed a great opportunity by not pass- ing to Hurst, who was just behind, and the Yorkshire forwards made another incursion into Lancashire's territory. The home forwards dribbled back to half-way and Brooke made a strong run in reply,but his final pass was not accepted. Eventually play settled down in neu- tral quarters,and time was soon afterwards called. Final result : Yorkshire,one goal and one try (eight points) ; Lancashire: nil. Teams : Lancashire, W. NORTRERN RUGBY UNION.

On Monday night a special general meeting of the Union was held at the George Hotel, Huddersfield, for the purpose of considering certain proposed amendments of the laws of the game.—Mr. J. E. Warren, Warrington, presided.—W. Nicholson, on behalf of the Halifax Club, referring to the proposed amendment of a rule which stood ha his name and which read as follows :—" The Rugby game of football as played by the Northern Rug- by Football Union should be played by 13 players on each side and to coasist of 6 forwards, 2 half backs, 4 three-quarter backs, and 1 full back," asked if he might be allowed to take the first part of the rule up to the word "side" and move that. He contended that it would lessen the expenses of travelling, insurance, and would be better for the half backs, referees, and specta- tors, and would have a tendency to dispense with a cer- tain amount of rough play. He held that fifteen players was too large a number, and it was thought by the great majority of people that thirteen would have a ten- dency to make the game more open.—Mr. Platt a ked Mr. Nicholls if he would withdraw the latter part of the resolution.—Mr. Nicholl assented, and Mr. Hutchity on seconded the resolution in its altered farm.—After dis- cussion the resolution was defeated by 18 to nine votes. —Mr. Nicholl then proposed to add ta rule 11 an addi- tional penalty in the shape of a free kick "for a deliber- ate knock-on."—Mr. Fattorini seconded, and it was carried by 22 to 4. In the Northern Union rules there is a rule in force that a half-back shall be considered off side if he gets into line with the last of his own forwards in a scrimmage.—Mr. Underwood (Wigan), now pro- posed an amendment to the effect that the word first be substituted for the word last in sub-section j, rule 11.— Mr. Fallorun (Nottingham), seconded the resolution.— Mr. Hutchinson strongly opposed the amendment. After discussion, only three voted for the amendment and it was lost.—Mr. oewell (Leeds) proposed a new rule to the effect that lines out should be abolished, and scrimmages ten yards from the line allowed instead. Mr. Jowett (Manningham) seconded the proposal, but after consi- dekable discussion, it was negatived by 18 votes to 10. Mr. Fallas (Wakefield) proposed that on a scrimmage being formed the ball should be put in on the side where the referee was standing. Mr. Smith (Widnes) seconded the proposal, and it was agreed to by a very large majority. This concluded the meeting. After tea a meeting of the Committee was held, Mr. H. Waller presiding. An application was made by J. Forsyth, late of the Huddersfield Club, to be allowed to play in Northern Rugby Union matches, and it was re- solved that the application be acceded to.

WARRINGTON " A " v. L. & N. W. Loco's hr.—These two local teams played on the Wilderspool ground on Saturday afternoon, before a few spectators. The visitors kicked off, and Foden com- menced to pass when the ball fell into the hands of one of the Loco men but Moores soon recovered it. Lawless got possession and made for the line, but slipped into touch. A good chance for Buckley was spoiled by him being unable to pick the ball up quick enough, and it was kicked into touch. The Loco's dribbled the ball down the fi-ld close to the line, but Spruce saved with a nice kick. Lawless made an attempt to drop a goal,but he slipped and the ball missed. Sudlow made a dash for

the line, but after successfully passing his opponents, he lost possession and before be had time to recover, the ball was rushed back. Price, Hoyle and Moores took the ball close to the Loco's line, where Buckley got over, Lawless took the kick and scored a goal. Savage ran through a crowd of his opponents and made a splendid run along the field, only to be stopped by Kenwright, who saved with a good kick. The Loco's began to press during the letter part of the first half, and prevented Warrington scoring anything further. On resuming, Warrington pressed and soon afterwards were awarded a free kick, which, however, gained them nothing. The Warrington men got away with a :rush and, hovering round the glal posts,a try seemed certain,Hockenhull re- lieved with a kick,which however,was smartly taken up by Moores,who dropped a goal. The reply of the locals was instrumental in bringing the ball close in upon Spruce,and flockenhull made his mark and with his kick struck the cross bar. The game ended with the score, Warrington two goals (one dropped), Loco's nil. The players %ere :—Warrington, full Lack, Spruce, three- quarters, Buckley, Scott,Lawless and Moores. half backs Foden and Price, forwards, Scholtze, Hoyle. Monks, Hughes,Swift, Savage, Bailey and Higham. L. and N.W. Railway Loco's :—Full back, Kenwright, three-quarters Potter, Powney, Hockenhull, and McHugh, half backs Holbro,.k, and Ravbould, forwards Sudlovv, Howard, Warburton, Llewellyn, Ashbrouk, Longworth, Cunning- ham and Taylor.

RUNCORN v. WIDNES " A "TEAMS—At Runcorn On Saturday in a violent hailstorm, which quite spoiled what would have been a keen game. There was only a handful of spectators on the grcund when the teams turned out. [he ability of either coucl not be gauged, owing to the conditions under which the match was played. The play on Saturday was only of a scrambling nature, the match ending in a draw, nothing being scored. The Runcorn team forwards displayed great improvement in combination, and will yet show well in the league tables.

AS OCIATION.

ST. ELPHIN'S V. ASPIILL CENTRAL.—At Wilderspoo Causeway on Saturday. The visitors won the toss, and Howard kicked off for the Saints, against a stiff wind. Aspull at once attacked, and through looseness of St. Elphin's backs, netted the ball in the first five minutes. This early success roused up both teams, and a splendid struggle was witnessed, the ball travelling rapidly from end to end, but the backs were generally good enough to check any efforts of the forwards, neither goal-keeper having many ,ifficult shots to stop. When the teams crossed over Aspull led by a goal. Aided by the wind the Saints now began to press, and had several ineffec- tual shies at goal, once when the goal was at their mercy shooting outside. The game proceeded on the same lines, very open, and neither side having much advant- age, though Duckworth netted the ball for the home team from a free kick, but unfortunately 'it had not touched a second player. The game ended Aspull, one goal ; Saints, nil. THELWALL V. STOCKTON BEATIL—The return match between the above clubs was brought off on Saturday at Thelvvall in stormy weather. Stockton Heath went with a weak team, and during the first half of the game the homesters had slightly the best of matters, the teams crossing over with a goal to the credit of 'Fhelwall. During the second half Stockton Heath sc•Nred twice, through Probert ond Fitwrgald, the game ending in a win for the visitors by two goals to one.

RUNCORN ASSOCIATION V. WARRINGTON ST. MARY'S.— These teams met at Runcorn on Saturday in bitter weather. Neither side were fully rept esented. The final result stood—Runcorn, seven goals ; Warrington, St. Mary's, one goal.

_ RUNCORN RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL.

The council met in the Board room at Dutton Work- house on Tuesday afternoon. Mr. Mclntyre, J.P., occupied the chair, and there were also present Messrs. T. Baxter, C.C., J. Brandreth, J. W. Knowles, H. E. Heywood, I. Wright, J. Stokes, E. Aaron, J. Weir, J. Darbvshire, S. Woodward,T. Lewis, E. Wailington,J. B. Chadwick, J. Spruce, W. Bradford, C. E. Davenport, W. Holt, C. Roylance, and Mr. J. Ashton (clerk), Mr. G. Ashton (deputy clerk), Dr. Adams (medical officer), Messrs. Diggle and Swinton (surveyors), and Mr. Far- rington (nuisance inspector).

THE FOOTPATH ACROSS THE LOCKS AT LATCHFORD.

The Parish Council of Grappenhall called the attention of the Rural Council to the want uf proper lighting and facilities of access to the public pathway across the Latchford locks of the Ship Canal.—Mr. Branford stated that the defunct Highway Board had a great deal of cor- respondence respe;ting the pathway. The Company were bound by Act of Parliament to keep a foot road across the locks for foot passeneers. But at present the public had either to get over some railings near the locks, or walk round.—lt was decided that the Clerk write to the Canal Comp my on the matter.—Mr. Bradford secoaded, and it was agreed to.

===== 0002601_18951214_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
RELSBY WATER SCHEME.

A letter was read from Messrs. Putts,Potts, and Gard- ner, solicitors, to the flelsby Water Company,intlmating that the directors were agreeable that the charges to be made for the supply of cottages should be:determined by the Local Government Board and the Company were willing to pay a sum not exceeding £5 towards the cost of the contribution.—The Clerk was instructed to write to the solicitors of the Company that their offer was not as originally arranged. The Authority would be unable to take action unless they had a scale of prices submitted to them previously.

===== 0002601_18951214_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
IMPROVEMENTS AT HELSBY.

A report of the committee to consider several matters at Heleby was submitted. They recommended that that part of the sewer from Ebenezer Terrace be piped, and that the sewer from Mrs. Pritchard's property be con- nected therewith. A deputation from the lielsby Athle- tic Club waited upon the committee, and they recom- mended that Mr. Diggle, the surveyor, be requested to prepare an estimate of the cost of filling up a deep cess- pool on the field they required, of laying a sewer, and if necessary constructing a settling tank at a point near to the railway, and that such estimate be submitted to the Athletic Club,and they be requested to state how far they would be prepared to meet the District Council with regard to the cost. With regard to the disused quarry, the Inspector was instructed to ascertain the owner,and give notice to abate the nuisance by the erec- tion of a fence. READJUSTMENT OF ()Mews' DUTIES.

The Chairman moved the adoption of the report of the committee appointed to consider the question of the readjustment of the duties of the Inspector of Nuisances and the surveyors. It showed that on the motion of Mr. Heywood, seconded Ly Mr. Woodward, and it was recommended that the Rural District of Runcorn be divided into two sub-districts for sanitary purposes,and that one of them be assigned to Mr. Farrington and the other to Mr. Swinton ; also that Mr. Swinton be relieved of the duties of surveyor of highways, and that Mr. Diggle be appointed surveyor of the whole district ; and that the proposals be submitted to the several parochial committees.---The recommendation was approved of.

THE SHIP CANAL SLUICES.

Mr. Aaron drew attention to the fact that it was the intention of the Manchester Ship Canal Company to apply for power to close the tidal sluices of the canal. It would seriously affect several townships in their union and he thought a committee should be appointed to con- sider the matter.—Dr. Adams said that the bill it passed would affect materially the sewers in some townships.— Mr. Hey wood said the Parish Councils of the townships affected had received notice of the intentions of the Ship Canal Company. The Parish Council of Frodsham re- ferred the matter to the District Council in the hope that steps would be taken to safeguard the interests of the townships affected. He proposed that the following form *committee to consider the question :—Messrs. Knowles, Mclntyre, Chadwick, Williams, Heywood, Brandreth, Wright, Baxter, Woodward, Stokes. and Aaron.—Mr. Chadwick seconded, and it was agreed to.

===== 0002601_18951214_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
MEDICAL OFFICER'S REPORT.

Dr. Adams reported that scarlet fever had become epidemic in Halton. Thirteen cases in all had been re- ported to him, affecting seven houses. The disease appeared mainly to have been propagated through school infection, and as it was so near Christmas holi- days, and several fresh cases had been reported since his last visit he recommended that the schools be clbsed until the usual holidays would commence. One case of scarlet fever had occurred at Sevenoaks, one at Batton, and one at Moore, one case of diphtheria had occurred at Frodsham Lordship, and three cases of scarlet fever had just been reported to him at Frodsham.—The report was adopted on the motion of Mr. Wright.

===== 0002601_18951214_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
THE HEALTH OF KiNGSLET.

Mr. Woodward wished to know whether a report had been prepared as to the general health and sanitary state of Kingsley, for which he gave notice at the last meeting.—Dr. Adams said he had forgotten all about it • —Mr. Farrington said as regarded the sanitary condi- tion it was as good as any of the townships in the Union. —Dr. Adams said he could say verbally that since the last epidemic of scarlet fever he had only once been to Kingsley.—Mr. Woodward said be would ask for a report to be presented at the next meeting. The reason he desired it was that a rumour had gone forth ; and was being diligently circulated that Kingsley was very un- healthy and in an unsanitary state. If it was not so, he thought a public refutation should be made.

===== 0002601_18951214_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
INSANITARY ROUSES AT WESTON POINT.

Mr. Farrington reported with reference to the privies on certain property at Weston Point. Be had seen the agent regarding them, and he promised to have them attended to at once. They were still in an unsanitary condition, and he suggested that the agent be given 48 hours notice to remedy the nuisance,and in the event of non-compliance, legal proceedings be taken,—Tho, report was adopted.—Mr. Aaron moved that notice be served, and spoke of the shocking condition the privies gwe in. —Mr. Chadwick seconded, and corroborated what had been said.

Medical authorities assert that crying is the chief aim best exercise for young children, and one hospital super- ntendent says that a healthful baby should cry three or our times a day at least,and from ten to fifteen minutes t a time.
