===== 0002601_18951214_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ALLEGED BURIAL SCANDAL.

Mr. Stokes gave a report of the committee appointed to consider the alleged burial scandal at Sutton. He said the members of the committee present were Mr. Wallington, Mr. Wright, and himself. A letter was re- ceived from Mr. Heywood stating his inability to be present in consequence of illness. The committee went fully ints the matter, and had faur witnesses before them. He was sorry to say they were unable to bring a report as one member did not agree, and that was Mr. Wallington. The statement that the body was laid in the coffin with a rope round it was without foundation. The chief complaint was that the body was buried with- out being washed. They found that to be the case, but upon inquiry there were reasons given for doing so. The Contractor made coffins for the workhouse and all he had to do was to make them and deliver them at the workhouse. Hie one at Sutton was a special case. He received information from Mr. Cooke to put the body in the coffin and wash it. It appeared that the man was very clean as regarded his bands and face, and as none of the relatives put in an appearance, the body was put in the coffin with the clothes on. The contractor had no option but to bury him with his clothes on or in a nude condition, as no expense was allowed by the authority for the purchase of a shroud. He (Mr. Stakes) thought it was time that the authority recommended that some provision be made for a shroud or something of that kind in such cases. He thought the contractor acted wisely under the circum- stances. But at the same time the contractor had received instructions that the body should be washed. There was another charge brought forward, but with respect te this the evidence given was rather contradic- tory.—Mr. Wallington said he quite agreed with what Mr. Stokes had said about the allegation respecting the rope being tied round the man. He (Mr. Wallington) had been misled with regard to that matter. The four witnesses who gave evidence almost all agreed in saying that the body was pressed into the coffin owing to it being too small, and that in consequence, the coffin was split on one side. A wisp of straw was put under the man's head for want of a hotter pillow, and not only that, but the undertaker did not measure the man for the coffin, and also forgot to bring some screws with him from Halton. When he got to the place with the coffin be asked if anyone had got some screws, or nails would do.—Mr. Yarwoed (in- terrupting) expressed the opinion that the statement was being far fetched. There were thousands of cases where the coffin lids were nailed down.—Mr. Wallington (continuing) said he did not think it was far fetched. One person had told him that she would have only been too willing to have laid the deceased out, and have found everything necessary. He thought it was a disgraceful manner in which the deceased was forced into the coffin. —Mr. Wright stated that the contractor got his in- formation respecting the size of the coffin from the police sergeant. He made the coffin as he thought, and he had told them that the coffin was too large. The man was not pressed in the coffin at all. Of the witnesses who said that the coffin was burst, one said it was on one side, and another on the other. Ile (Mr. Wright) saw the Rev. Dr.Gastaldi who buried the man, and he told him that he saw no marks on the coffin. He also saw Mr. Yarwood's man who drove the hearse, and who had plenty of time to see the coffin, and he said he saw no cracks. He (Mr. Wright) did not blame Mr Wallington for bringing the matter forward, for if his informants told the truth there was certainly a scandal. That about the rope was certainly not true, and as to having a wisp of straw under the man's head,he saw no- thing objectionable in that. With regard to the man being buried in his clothes, there were hundreds buried in that manner.—Mr. Wallington said they had four witnesses before them, and two or three of them took their oath that the coffin was split on one side.—Dr. Gastaldi sal he saw nothing wrong with the coffin: it looked a decen one, and in no way a disgrace.—Mr. Baxter wished to know why the deceased was buried at a greater expense than any ordinary pauper. He understood it cost some- thing over £3 to bury the man, in consequence of treat- ing him other than an ordinary pauper He thought the man might have been buried at Ashton or Little Leigh.—Mr. Heywood suggested and ultimately moved that a letter be sent from the Board to the relieving offi- cers and undertaker,calling their attention to the matter and asking them to ' exercise a little more caution in future with regard to pauper burials.—Mr. Woodward seconded.—Mr. Wallington moved as an amendment that another committee be formed to inquire into the matter. —Mr. Brandreth thought that no charge had been brought against the relieving officer.—Mr. Stokes seconded the amendment.—Mr. Baxter said their re- lieving officer was exempt from blaine,and If the resolu- tion were passed it would look as if he had done some- ' thing wrong.—Mr. Roylance having cited a similar burial case at Moore some time ago, the resolution was carried.—Mr. Wallington expressed the opinion that the ratepayers of Sutton would not be satisfied with the way the matter had been treated. This concluded the business.

TOWN COUNCIL NOTES.

Actually the first discussion that took place at the last Town Council meeting of Warrington was Upon a subject that was not" on the minutes." It transpired, however, that there was sufficient explanation for the unexpected indulgence of the Mayor in tolerating a seemingly informal debate. This particular subject was, it is true,uot mentioned •on the printed minutes, but it appears that there are minutes and minutes connected with the Com- Mittees of Warrington Town Council. Some of the Minutes are printed and made public, others are not printed and are as a rule kept private. It was a subject mentioned in one ot the unprinted minutes of the Finance and Estate Committee that Alderman Sutton referred to when he alluded to the tempor- ary lettin:. for business purposes of the shop at the foot of Bridge-street which was formerly occupied by the late R. W. Marsh, but which has now been acquired by the Corporation. The Alderman argued that the re-lettiug of these premises by the Finance Com- Mittee was unfair to tradesmen in the immediate neighbourhood and he urged that it should be dis- continued. This view of the matter was strongly supported by Councillor Davies and Counciitur Pierpoint. The former seemed inclined to charge the Corporation with a breach ot faith with the tradesmen who bought laud in Bridge-street after the widening of tile street, because they had not Pulled Marsh's shop down before now ; and Mr. Pierpoint thought the Corporation was acting a rather unjust and mean part in encouraging " mock auctions ' for the sake of making three pounds a 'week. . The committee found defenders in Coun- Cillor Ilephero and Councillor Shaw Green, the Chairman. Mr. Ilepherd said the delay in pulling the shop down was due to what he vaguely termed a "financial matter," and Councillor Green took the ground that the committee were, so long as they had the property under their control, bound to make the best of it in the interests of the rate- Payers. In the end the action of the committee Was approved by the Council.

The subject which ilex.. excited the talkative faculties of some of the councillors was the pro- posal ot the Sanitary Works Committee to borrow ,435,000 for extensions at Longford depot. Mr. ' Monk-, the chairman of the committee, explained that it was not intended to 1168 the money at once, but that the application for power to borrow hat to be made to tile Local Government Board now, or a delay of twelve months would be csused.He added that there would have to be a Local Government Board inquiry into the application, and that nothing would be spent before a detailed Scheme of the extension had been laid before the Council. Counciller Smethurst then again assumed the familiar role of the austere euonumist. tie did not deny that extensions were necessary at Longford, nor that money would have to be spent in making those extensions, but the round sum of £15,000 was evidently more than he could swallow all at once. He pleaded that it bight b- cut down to £lO,OOO, because he had found from experience that committees had a fatal habit ut sprii,A I lig all that they had power to spend, and more sometimes. Councillor Woods objected to £75 a piece being given for horses, but later on Councillorßoberts in dealing with this point argued that good horses were the cheapest in the end,and be suggested that the Warrington Corporation, like some other Corporations, should take a pride in the horses it turned out. In dealing with Mr. Sme- thurst's speech,Mr. Roberts pointed out that owing to the increasing demands made upon the Long- ford Depot, extensions were absolutely necessary. Councillor Monks having replied upon the debate, _ _ .

and again assured the Council that no money would be spent without its sanction, the minutes of the Committee were confirmed.

As might have been expected a matter of so muck importance as the proposed new sewer to deal with the sewerage of Stockton Heath was not allowed to pass without comment. Councillor Davies spoke Modestly and discreetly but firmly upon this ques- tion. He did not like the idea of bring- ing the sewage of Stockton Heath into the boroir4h for the purpose of being dis- charged into the river, and he had a counter scheme of his own which he explained. though the explanation was not conspicuously lucid. He was

tot sure whether his own scheme was prac- ticable or not, and be was quite prepared to be satisfied with the borough surveyor's Plan if it was the only practicable one. Both Coun- cillor Roberts and Councillor Monks also saw objec- tions to the proposal of the cDnimittee, chiefly on the ground that the outlet would be near to houses at River-road,and they suggested that some further consideration might be given to the matter. An explanation was afterwards given by the Borough surveyor, who pointed out the objections to the alternative schemes, and after Alderman Wright tad stated that the Council were not definitely Pledged to any plan the subject dropped.

'The business ot the meeting was brought to a Close with a lively little debate over the resolution of the General Purposes Committee to prohibit the use of any part of the Drill Hall for committee rooms at elections. Alderman Sutton said the re solution was directed against the use of a room by the Conservatives, and he moved that it be not con- firmed by the Council. The amendment was seconded by Mr.Smethurst, who declared that the Liberals might use another room in the Hall, hut the Mayor discounted this amiable suggestion by the remark that half-a-dozen-policemen would be necessary to look after the rival parties. The debate was continued in an amicable spirit, the ro os t pointed contribution being that of Councillor Plimpton, who declared that in at least one in- stance he knew of a voter going direct from the polling booth into the Conservative committee rooms. In the end Alderman Sutton withdrew his aniendment and the resolution was passed.

tENEFIT NIGHT AT THE WARRING-

TON CIRCUS.

PRESENTATION TO MR. OFIMY. On Wednesday last week there was a crowded attend- 'Mee at Mr. Ohmy's Circus,Queen-street, as the occasion Was set apart for the benefit of the popular proprietor. itn exceptionally good programme had been compiled and everything had been done to make the occasion the auccess which it turned out to be. Among, those present Were the Mayor and Mayoress and Mr. T. alorris. The lengthy programme commenced with one of Mr. Met- calfe's self-composed overtures, after which Claud Iphrny displayed several riding feats. Two clowns, the brothers Almers,gave a wonderfully dexterous perform- ance with a large felt sombrero. Miss Lizzie put the Derforming horse •'Birkenhead " through his paces and Young Bonfantie performed on the tight wire in an accomplished manner. The mad poet on horseback was acted in an amusing manner by Mr. Walker, and Mons. laYados as a juggler per formed tricks of dexterity with. Out end, a number of them being with a 221 b cannon- Miss Ada Rooke displayed graceful barebacked V.uing, while Dickey, the clown, had a comic act all to unnself. An amusing feature of the entertainment was the introduction to the ring of an unrideable mule which 17,..atious youthful members of the audience endeavoured master—of course without success. The brothers Altneros gave a skilful globe-roiling show, following Which Mr. Ohmy performed his sensational mid-air feats tnd was presented with a handsome bouquet. Richmond .ttnii, a bare-backed rider of repute also performed, and olr. Harry Dale, the popular prince of jesters recited a Weverly-cornposed combination of the signs of the hotels and public-houses in Warrington. Clement Scott's s' Dover Express" was als,s delivered by him in an elo leent manner. Perhaps the most striking feature of the Whole entertainment was the acrobatic display by the Leopoldo, a troupe of nine lady and gentlemen acrobats, atYled with some reason " the best show on earth," for their skill and strength is undoubted. Miss Sheridan lanced very nicely a Highland jig. Other items not 119Wn on the programme, but for which prizes were Riven, were a sack race, and duck hunt by blindfolded n's. Prizes were also won by the lady with the longest nair in the circus, the oldest man, the finest baby, thf lallest man, the smallest man, and the ugliest man, the .atter being won by a laughing negro. During the per- lOrmance Mr. Ohmy was presented by Messrs. Twig and S'ey, of Manchester, with a diamond ring, as a re- Ognition of his courtesy, gentlematiliness, and strict attention to business. Mr. Ohmy returned thanks to th( PeaYer and Mayoress and the large audience far their at. ndanee at his benefit, and he also mentioned with l'egret that this was his last week in Warrington, as he Was shortly departing for Birkenhead.

MISSIONARY MEETING AT LATCH FORD.

On Thursday last week a missionary meeting was hdd at Latchford Wesleyan Church,Counciller Henry Roberts presiding. There was a poor attendance, the weather being decidedly unfavourable. There were on the plat- form in add.tion to the Chairman,the Revs. G. D. Mason, H. Walmsley, Mr. John Hartley, and Mr. W. A. Sand- ford.

The Rev. G. D. Mason, after introducing the chairman, read the financial statement of the Society in which he stated that the income of the current account for the last year was £125,806 12s. Bd.' and the total expendi- ture £127,457 lie. 2d., leaving a deficiency tor the year of £1650 18, 6.1. and this deficiency had accumulated to £3478 15s. 10d., but in the same breath he might say tlaat the whule of it had been more than wiped off by sub- scriptions—(applause)—and considerably more tidan £lOO had been contributed spontaneously by the Warr- ington circuit. Tile total income for this circuit was £248,08. ld. of which the Latchford branch contributed £5 2s. (Applause.) . .

The Chairman said he was afraid the inclemency of the weather might have had some effect in keeping some of their friends away from the meeting. What they lacked in numbers however they must make up to some extent in enthusiasm, because it would not do let the great cause they had come to support suffer from the unavoidable absence of some of their un- doubted friends. (Hear, hear.) He %vas pleased to find from the very short acquaintance he had had with Mr. Walinsley, that he was the son of a very old friend of his, and he was glad to see that Warrington had sent out a man in the person of Mr. Welinsley who had worked in that great cause so many many miles away from home. It was sad but it was none the less the fact that in spite of the magnificent results which missionary work had attained in almost all quarters of the world, that they found almost the same objections to it as at the beginning. Many good people said that there was so much work to be done at home that they had neither men nor money to spare for work abroad. It was true that there was work to be done at home, but when we remembered the tact that this coun- try of ours owed its splendid position to the early mis- sionaries, we should be guilty of almost contemptible ingratitude if we were to allow ourselves to be satisfied with what we had done. He had always noticed that many of the people who raised that objection were not in them ,elves the most active in doing the work at home. It was often the case too that those who had taken the most active interest in the affairs of our own country, and in its moral and spiritual elevation, were those who took the greatest interest in those who were far away from us. (Hear, hear.) Another objection that he sometimes met with was that some missionaries were not altogether taithful to their duties ; in other words that their career was one of rather more ease than we had hitherto been led to believe it was. There was a percentage of such men in all branches of life,and there might have been a solitary instance of a missionary who had fallen away from the work for which he was sent out. That was no reason whatever that the great missionary cause should be held up to the contempt and derision it was in some quarters. When they looked back upon the work of such men as Living- stone, Moffatt,and many others, he said it was nothing short of a gross libel upon the noble men who were carry- ing on the work, to say that that was true generally of the missionaries leaving this country for foreign parts. (Hear, hear, and applause.) The children of mission- aries in many parts of the world had to be sent home for education and health for years,so that when they re- turned they had grown almost out of the knowledge of their parents. That in itself was a great testimony to the spirit of self-sacrifice which animated the men who went out to serve the Master such a great distance from home. It was the duty of every man who took upon himself the profession of a Christian to do what he could to further the missionary cause. (Hear, hear, and applause.) Mr. Sandford said that to his mind missionary work was one of the greatest and most noble causes we had , before us. I taught us our duty to all mankind, who, in Christ's view, were all our brethren. 1 The Rev. denry Walmsley, of Belize, Honduras, said he was glad to be at Latchford because it was in the old Temperance Hall that he made his first attempt at preaching. He was glad to be under the chairmanship of Mr. Roberts who had given to them the most intelligent chairman's speech he had heard since he arrived in Eng- land. The district where he had been working for the last six years was one about which not so much was known as about others. There were three races of people there and it was among them thatthis work had gone on. First, among the black people who were exactly the same kind of people as inhabited the West, Indian Hands at the present time and their work among them was precisely the same also. Belize was the capital of British Honduras and in the two circuits in the coun- try the black people predominated. At the present time in Belize, a town of 8,000 or 10,000 people there were two ' Methodist churches at which together about 1,000 people , worshipped every Sabbath. The gospel had taken hold of them to such an extent that there were 500 or 600 Christian members of the churches. There were two Wesleyan ministers in Belize and the income frOm their circuits met all the expenditure in connection with them. Year by year for some time past there had been raised in the circuit no less a sum than £lOO English money which had gone to other parts of the district. Their work ameng the black people had justified itself by the results it had produced. Another race was that of the Caribs,of whom few remained. At one time they were cannibals,and he believed that up to comparatively recent times that habit prevailed among them. In the Carib town there was also a church and the circuit con- tributed £9O annually to the support of its own ministers. That was not a bad result of 20 years work. The third class of people was that of the Spanish Indians. Among them was the hardest field in which they had to work ,as wherever the Spanish power had been, Roman Catholic- ism had gone with it and the people protested that they had the Christian religion and would not change. After a collection had been taken a vote of thanks was passed to the chairman and speakers on the motion of the Rev. G. D. Mason. .

-..., . - . -. -.........-. The singing of the doxology and the pronouncing of the benediction closed the meeting.

CHILD NEGLECT AT WARRINGTON.'

TWO CASES.

Before Messrs. Chandley,Ridgway, and Sutton, at the Warrington Borough Police Court, on Thursday week, Catherine Richardson, 38, Haydock-st., Warrington,was charged with neglecting her child, Thomas, on the 22nd November, and divers other dates during the past six months, in such a manner as to cause him unnecessary suffering.—lnspector M'Ateer said the child, Thomas whom defendant was charged with neglecting was aged, two years.On the 23rd of November he went in company with P.C. Meacock to the defendant's house and found her and her husband and the child and the daughter Elizabeth at home. Defendant was drunk and using abominable language,and she struck her husband in the face with her fist. The house was filthy, and the beds unfit to lie in. On the 22nd of November he saw her ontside in the rain with the baby, which was saturated, and the defendant was carrying it head downwards. She was a perpetual drunkard, and in addition to the child being illegitimate she was paid for its support. He asked the Bench to deal severely with the case.—P.C. Meacock said he had visited the house in company ,vith lospector M'Ateer and he found the defendant drunk. The baby was tltting in the corner, and she picked it up but after some remark to M'Ateer she put it down by the fire but fell against it and knocked it on the fender. Her husband picked it up and took care of it. She struck him and used very obscene language.— P.C. Evans said that he had known the defendant eight or ten years,and she was a woman addicted to drink. At 12.30 on the 27th August he locked her up for being drunk and disorderly in Taniaer's-lane.—Thomas Rich- ardson, husband of defendant, said that his wife started drinking about the year 1885 and she had been drinking ever since. Daring the last six months she had been hardly ever sober. She was drunk on the 19th, 22nd, 23rd, and 28th of November.—Sergeant Chantler said that about two months ago he was serving a summons on defendant and found her at home in company with four other womentand all five were drunk. The husband was a steady, sober man.—William Bold, witness on behalf of defendant, said that when she was sober there vas not a better neighbour. he,hewever,admitted that ihe was drunk at times.—The magistrates committed tier to gaol for two months hard labour. Samuel Beckett and Annie Beckett, 28, Warburton-st., were charged with having on the 21st November and ither dates during the past six months, wilfully neg- lected their children, Albert and Samuel Beckett. Both pleaded not guilty.—lnspector Witeer said that in con- iequence of a complaint he Visited the defendant's house m the 21st of last month in company with P.C. Wood .nd saw there the male defendant and the youngest boy, iamuel. They examined the child and found that it had in a ragged pair of" pants " and no shirt. It also ap- peared to be starving with cold. In the front room lownstairs there was no furniture and very little in the citchen. There was no fire and little food, while in the )edroom was a bed in a filthy and ragged condition, in act," a respectable dog would not lie on it." He had ilso visited the house in company with Sergeant Chantler nd found no improvement and the woman had told him io alterations were necessary. Be had watched the )eople and had come to the conclusion that their chit- lren were grossly and systematically neglected at night .nd all hours of the day.—P.C. Wood corroborated, and •emarked that the stench in the house was so bad that is opened the windows back and front himself.—Str- :sant Chantler also supported the evidence but did not link Beckett was a drunken man.—Samuel Beckett,the nale defendant said, in defence, that for the last three nonths he had had little work, and for six weeks his :ife had been in the Infirmary. He did his best for the hildren but he could not keep the house clean. Be had .owever got regular work to go to and promised faith• ully to amend.—Mr. Chandley said that under the cir- umstances, though the case had been a4journed before, .e would grant defelsdant another month, at the same ,me warning him that if there was no improvement he 'would have to go to gaol.

?Lettero to the etittor.

THE RECENT SUNDAY CONCERTS IN PARR BALL, WARRINGTON. Sir,—ln a local paper recently a writer made the following misleading remarks :—" I should like very much to understand the difference between a sacred coacert given at the Parr Hall and the performances given every Sunday afternoon at the Wyclitr Chapel and the Y.M.C. A. and called "Pleasant Sunday After- noons. In one case, lam informed that a charge of one penny each is made for admission to these enter- tainments. It is said that the money is returned to the people who attend, in the shape of books for the best attendance." I can clearly see by these statements that the writer knows nothing of the P.S.A. movement. No charge is made for admission to the Wycliffe Pleasant Sunday Afternoon services. We have a prizo system to which members pay a penny a week (and which has been the means of introducing good books into many households, which might otherwise have been without), but it is optional whether you join it or not. To contrast the recent Sunday concerts with the P.S.A.rnovement is altogether out of reason. These concerts were got up merely for money-making purposes, but the P.S.A. movement is an endeavour to get w..rkingmen into the churches and into closer companionship with Christ. It is unsectarian,and Churchmen as well as Nanconfornaists take part in the service.—Yours truly, A WYCLIPFE P.S.A. MEMBER.

===== 0002601_18951214_art0136.txt | included=true | included =====
MINISTERS AND THEIR TITLES.

Sir.—The pages of "The Examiner" from time to time afford evidence that Mr. Edward T. Reed of the institu- tion known as Emmanuel Church is very anxious to gain a position in the town,and to impress upon the minds of cur civic authorities and the public that he is a "Minis- ter of Christ." He does not, however, so far as I knew, offer a particle of proof in support Jf his claim to this ministry. I am, of course, aware that he has acquired the amount of book learning enjoyed by his sect, that he dons the proper clerical habiliments, attaches the word reverend to his name, enjoys a comfortable position, and makes his prayers and his sermons in orthodox fashion. NOW if these beggarly elements of the world are to be regarded as the true signs of Apostleship then indeed Mr. Reed must be admitted to the roil ot Chris- tian ministers. But when we come to examine the Book which the pastor of Emmanuel and others like him pro- fess to adopt as their standard in matters of faith and practice, we are perfectly astounded to find that there is no resemi,lance whatever between the modern and the Primitive signs of Apostleship. The first ministers chosen and ordained by the founder of Christianity were humble and obscure men who had no training in schools of theology, who were content to clothe them- selves in the garments of their class, and who knew nothire, about titles and emoluments. Yet it was to these bumble men that Jesus gave commission to preach his gospel to every creature ; and that they might not fail in their great and laborious work he endowed them with supernatural gilts, such as the discerning of spirits, power to heal the sick, ability to speak in all the lang- uages of the world, and to preach with the power and demonstration of the spirit of God. Armed with these powers they went forth on their mission ; and in less than kalf a century little congregations of believers were gathered together in every quarter of the Roman world. But it is worthy of note that their Master made no pro- vision for their physical wants. Nor was it necessary for Hist to do so ; for had they not lived with hi m,shared his poverty and persecutioo, and learned to look to Him and to Him alone for all their requirements. When Mr. Reed can show us that he is possessed of these signs of apostleship I shall gladly recognise him as a minister of Christ, and regard him as a " beloved brother Edward." But until then his utterances, whether in the pulpit, or through the press, will be,to one at least, nothing bitter than the sounding of brass, or the ticklings of a cymbal. —Yours truly, .

===== 0002601_18951214_art0137.txt | included=true | included =====
MISSIONARY APOLOGETICS.

Sir,—The very air of late has been foil of missionary talk and talk about missionaries ; and in this talk I noticed that there is a prevailing undertone of mission- ary apologetics—a defence of the utility and benefit of missionary work. Now so much protestation must have a cause. Can it be that the missionaries and pro-mission- aries are beginning themselves to doubt the utility of carrying religious frying-pans and blankets to Timbuc- too ? Are they beginning to see—what the unregenerate outsider has long contended—that it would be better to settle exactly what we ourselves believe, ere we enter upon the ungracious task of teaching others wiaat we have discarded ourselves? The missionaries of last century went forth with undoubting zeal and told un- conscious cramm as to the age of the world, assigning to it a date when we have evidence of our own country being peopled ! But Mr. Missionary was cock-sire about it. Adam and Eve were no myths to him,nor the Genesis account of Creation "unhistorical" as it is to the Church Congress. He was even more cer- tain of the actual truth of the Chaldce in fable than the "Rev. Professor Thoumaian,"who thought that Armenia was " probably " the site of the Garden of Eden ! Per- sonally I should feel more certain of this had the Rev. Professor brought some undoubted relic over.

- I wonder it has never occurred to people who are afflicted with tbe missionary mania that it i rather anomalous to:be sending folk fram here to teach the Chinese the maxim " Do unto others as you would they should do to you," seeing that the maxim itself came from China ! Rather like taking coals to Newcastle or teaching your grandmother to suck eggs ! By the way, how would it be if these China missionaries and their English friends put this very excellent moral precept into practice? "Do as you would be done by." Ex- cellent ! Then keep out of China my friends ; don't go there and insult the people by reviling their religion. How would you welcome a Chinaman who was infatu- ated enough to come among you and sought to enlighten your benighted souls by inculcating the mysteries of Buddha and the: sacredness of ancestor-worship ; who erected a pagodalvrithin sight and hearing of your little bethels ; and sought by fair meows or foul to entice the lambs of your flock to become the proselytes of their faith ? would; not the Chinese maxim fit this (quite suppositious) breach ot Chinese good taste and good manners ? But perhaps we have one code of morals and good taste for curselvea and quite another for the be- nighted infidels, who were well advanced in learning and civilization when our ancestors were woaded savages. What wonder that China looks askance at us when we send tasteless fanatics to teach them the religious offal of the last century ? And this too with the added insult of a gunboat backing! The British taxpayer will be wise to let the missionary tub stand on its own bottom —if it has 0110.—Yours, &c.,

JOHN PEERS. THE ALLEGED ENCROACHMENTS AT BANK

QUAY.

Sir,—lf Mr. R. T. Fairclough had contented himself with addressing the public through the medium of your columns upon the question of Me9srs. Bishop and Co.'s Wharf only, I would not have troubled you with any observations upon his letter of Saturday last, but when he goes out of his way to make accusations by inuendo against people whose reputation for integrity and un- selfishness, is at least quite as good as his own. lam compelled to reply. So far as Mr. R. T. Fairclough's conduct in regard to Messrs. Bishop and Co.'s proposals is concerned, the electors in Fairfield Ward have in so pronounced a manner expressed their views upon their late representative's action that I need not further refer to it.

The last paragraph in Mr. Fairclough's letter contains a statement which suggests that the wharf which the Warrington Slate Company has occupied for eight years past has been acquired in an improper manner,and that it was at one tine a "free public wharf." Let me say IS passing that it is very singular that Mr. R. T. Fair- slough has only thought it worth his while to call the !attention of the publie to these circumstances since he Involuntarily retired from the Council, although the Wharf has been tenanted by my firm fur the period al- ready nam -A. The wharf is owned by two firms, viz., Messrs. J. Crosfield and Sons and Messrs. EI.D. Pochin,te whom we pay rent. Does Mr. R. T. Faircicugh suggest that either of these firms has acquired this wharf im- properly ? He knows perfectly well that he dare not make any such suggestion. Further, that portion of the wharf whish belongs to Messrs. Crosfield and Sons has been in ,their possession for at least 80 years and the other half, has belonged to Messrs. Pochin and their predecessors, also for a very long period. Again the wharf never was a public or a free wharf, and the in- cident referred to by Mr. Fairclough as to the discharg- ing of "grain free of toll " at the wharf, occurred at a time when Mr. Fairclough's father rented the old Pottery from Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons,and bad the privi- lege of discharging grain there in consequence. I would strongly advise Mr. R. T. Fairclough in future to take more trouble in investigating the accuracy of his sup- posed facts before rushing into print. But, Sir, Mr. R. T. Fairclough is the last man who should talk about the absorption of the rights of the public. Soon after he and his partners became the owners of Littons Mill, they sent plans to the Corpora- tion for building over the footpath and cartway which goes past the side of the mill and over the railway crossings through Quay Fold into Liverpeol-road. Mese plans were rejected more than once because it was believed the building proposed t) be erected would have trespassed upon the rights of the public, and I recollect very well in one interview with the Street Improvement Committee, that Mr. John Fairclough threatened the Corporatien with litigation if the plans were not passed. They are not, however, passed yet, but I noticed the other day that a chain and posts had been fixed across the cartway to prevent the public from having vehicular access to the route referred to. I believe strongly that the public have a right to the cartway in question and in support of that opinion I may mention the fact that my firm has carted large quantities of material over the road without the slightest hindrance until Messrs. Fairclough became the owner of the mill at Bank Quay. Finally, seeing that Mr. R. T. Fairclough is no longer a member of the Council, why does he not resign the position he holds as the representative of the Corpora- tionpi numb public i oi nn t?e r e I:toss syi si bpl prompting newly discovered nnghliys disconvnertehde zeal rfSoeYr thCe° present unselfish (?) conduct in sticking to the post.— Yours faithfully, H. ROBERTS. •

MEET OP THE PABGATE 9CIIOOL.

The minutes of the Schools Committee were moved by Mr. DAVIES and seconded by Mr. PILLING.—The Rev. J. H. RIDDETTE moved that the minutes be adjourned until the end of the meeting, because in the presence of the reporters he did not want to be obliged to ask certain questions, which would be bettar asked in committee. He had given private notice to the Clerk of this motion, and he hoped that the Beard would agree to the minutes be- ing adjourned until the end of the meeting.—Mr. Hick- man seconded.—Mr. Knowles said that he thoughtthat the motion was an extraordinary one, and never, so long as he had been a member of the Board, had they been asked to postpone the minutes until the business of the Board had been completed. He was not present at the last meeting of the committee, and if there was anything done then wrong, he was sorry that he was absent. He felt strongly that as the minutes had been proposed and seconded the Guardians should agree to them. Mr. Riddette might want to raise a storm in a teapot, but he felt bound to vote that the minutes be confirmed, and he felt sure the other mem- bers must feel the same. If Mr. Riddette had some- thing to bring up about what bad taken place behind the scenes let him do so. He thought that the committee ought to have the confidence of the Board.—Mr. liwnitrrE denied that there was any question of want of confidence in the committee.—The Cstateraen said that if Mr. Knowles persisted he must put the amendment to the vote.—Mr. KNOWLES : I don't think any out- side member ought to be allowed to question the man- agement P—Mr. RIDDETTE said that he was accustomed to the veiled abuse of Mr. Knowles and was getting inured to it. The course he had proposed did not put any reflection upon the committee, but he had taken it out of consideration for the persons concerned. He had not referred to the committee,nor was it his intention to propose any censure upon the committee. All he wanted was for the minutes to be postponed until the close of the meeting. If they were not, he would have to ask four questions which would of course go into the papers, and it was desirable that they should be asked in corn- ruittee.—The amendment was then put, and only six voting for it, it fell to the ground.—The CLERK, address- ing Mr. Riddette, said that the only question before the Board was whether the minutes as read should be adopted, and therefore any question discussed should have immediate reference to them.—Mr. Ituaranrrn said that he accented the Clerk's ruling, but the minutes were not sufficiently correct with regard to the pro- ceedings of the meetings reported upon. He would now ask the questions to which he had referred. He would ask in the first place whether Mr. Jonner Fust had paid a surprise visit with two other officials of the Local Government Board to the Padgate schools. Further. whether he had not sent in a report reflecting in a very painful way upon the management of that institution. Also whether it was true that the Sihools Committee had considered that report at a meeting at which only five Guardians were present; aed whether also it was true that the master of the school had been censured by the committee, and one member of the staff asked to resign. He was extremely sorry that he should have had those questions dragged out of him. He took the precaution in all kindness by sending a private note to the Clerk, and he had hoped that there would have been no necessity for questions to be asked in public. He hoped that they would now see that he had been justified in taking the course he had taken that morning. The minutes received were not a sufficient record, and many members of the Board had not had the remotest idea of what had taken place. He woulcl like to say that he laid the responsibility of having to ask the questions upon his friend, Mr. Knowles. He (Mr. Riddette) was not a member of the Padgate School Committee but he WAS a Guardian, and had a joint re- sponsibility with all—(hear, hear,)—and he was not go- ing to throw away that responsibility on the plea worthy of an attorney, that he was only a member of the Board and not a member of the Schools Committee. He wished an answer to all his questions.—Mr. 13maNciz thought t',at the best course to adopt would be to refer the questions to the Schools Committee, and have them dis- cussed at the next meeting of the committee, at the same allowing Mr. Riddette and other members of the Board to be present at the meeting.—Mr. RIDDETTE thought that he was entitled to an answer. He appealed to the chairman of the Schools Committee for a categori- cal answer to the questions.—Mr. DAVIES said that he might tell Mr. Ritidette that it was quite true they had a surprise visit from Mr. Jenner Fust and Inspector Mocr- soul. It was quite true that they had left a report, but that report was for the exclusive use of the committee, and that committee had gone minutely into that report. At that moment, he thought the Board had better leave the matter whore it stood. He could assure Mr. Riddette that very strong language had been used by the com- mittee to the people who were in authority in that estab- lishment, and he could also further tell him tnat unless a very great reform was made, the committee were pre- pared to take extreme measures. He hoped that for the present, Mr. Riddette would leave the matter where it stood . Mr. RinnErre laid that he would not have taken the course he did had there been any indication of what had taken place upon the minutes. He thanked Mr. Davies for his courteous answer.—The CLERK said that he would like it to be known that all visits of Local Government Board inspectors were to some extent surprise visits. They were never by notice. With regard to the statement thot some officer was called upon to resign it only amounted to this, that when the report was read at the meeting of the committee, during the conversation, it was felt that the superintendent shculd be informed that unless there was proper explanation and remedial steps were taken, it would inevitably follow that one if not ' two officials might be called upon to resign. The medical officer had been asked to give a report and that gentleman had done so, and that report had been con- sidered by the committee. That could be read if neces- sary.—Mr. RIDDETTE : I think both reports should be read.—The CLERK said that he could not do it.—Mr. Rinnwrre said that he was quite willing for the medical officer's report to be read provided Mr. Jenner Fust's report were read also.—Mr. Dams : I must object to that.—Mr. DICKMAN thought that it would be better at that stage to proceed to the next business and leave the question for the present.—Mr. RIDDETTE : I am quite willing to allow the matter to drop if you will not read either report.—The subject then dropped.

CHILDREN'S ENTERTAINMENT AT NEWTON.

For the sixth rr in succession Mr. Singleton, head master of St. Peter's schools. Newton, put be- fore the public at the Town Hall, Newton, on Satur- day evaliag the excellent entertainment which costs him and his assistants natich timb and labour to produce. As in previous years he has pressed into service a good number of scholars attending his schools who show aptitude for the work, and with a great aracfunt of training are enabled to Ap- pear on a public platform in a presentable manner. There was a crowded audience on Saturday night, who greatly appreciated the bill, of fare the youngsters pat before them. Not only were the elder scholars requisitioned, but the merest infants were put on the platform to take their part in the evening's entertainment. The programme was something similar to those produced in previous years, one portion, that appertaining to the infants.being under the care of Wes Liptrott, and the other of Mr. Singleton and Mr. A. E. Wykers. The rising of the curtain displayed the whole of the children on the capacious platform, and the intermingling of the van-coloured dresses and costumes presented a really pretty appearance. Tho programme was opened with a school song which was executed in excellent time and with precision, the blending of the voices being charming in the extreme. The next item was a recitation by Mabel Turton who bemoaned the loss of her doll which was supposed to be dead. The infants next, in song. illustrated " The trades." those depicted being the blacksmith, cobbler, and miller. In this three or four children represented a par- ticular trade, whilst the chorus sang the ac- companiment. Th's was very cleverly done, and elicited warm appreciation. An entire change was now made, the centre of the platform being given over to a juvenile nigger troupe in charge of Mr. A.E.Wykes, assistant-master. The lads were carefully dressed to represent a full-fledged troupe of minstrels, and their singing and dialogues were much enjoyed. There were 18 performers and the choruses were capitably sung. Mr. A. Wykes sang " Susan Jane," his brother sang " Maggie," and one of the troupe gave a good interpre- tation ot "The Dandy-coloured coon." The dialogues were well presented, and the local his which were intro- duced were enjoyed by the audience. Again the infants were in evidence with a musical drill, with wands and dumb-hells, and their next item was termed infants' play, " Dr. Brooks" being a dialogue in which the central figure was a sick child, surrounded by its grand- mother, mother and sisters, the part of the doctor being naively taken by Master Singleton. The concluding item for the infants was the action song " Busy men." The second part of the programme was the kinderspiel by J. C. Grieve, entitled " Playmates." This is a very simple affair but by reason of its quaintness was ren- dered most interesting, the costumes adding additional interest. The characters represented in it were Mother Goose (the oldest resident of the village of Whaurist), Miss M. Baybut ; Aunt Sally (Mother Goose's younger sister and maid-af-all-work;, Amy Crompton ; Flora Macdonald (Annie Brodie), Annie Laurie (Annie Ellam), Maggie Lander (Annie Bethel!), holiday visitors at the village of Whaurist ; Jack Goose (only son of Mother Goose), John Greenslade; Farmer Jack (afterwards Jack the Giant killer), Torn Gee ; Dick Whittington ' (afterwards Lord Mayor of Lond ,n) W. Strange ; Cow- boy Jack (afterwards Jack and the Bean Stalk), William Burgess ; Simple Simon (the village natural ; occasion- ally taken for a flat, but being sometimes decidedly sharp)„W. Haynes. Herahle,Sam uel Siddall, and Ernest Weeks ; Highlanders, Frank Gee, Tom Cook, Frank , Fletcher, Hobert Campbell, Harold Mayor and Charles Mayor. Chorus, composed of Jack Goose's playmates. The scene lay near the cottage of Mother Goose in . the village of Whaurist, exact locality unknown. Jack at the outset tells his playmates of the former poverty of himself and mother, until he found a goose which laid a golden egg, and this was the turning point in their poverty, and the various scenes really hinge on this, although they lead up to the introduction of the other characters in the representations. The singing was re- markably good, as also was the acting, in fact it would be invidious to single out any particular one for special mention, when all did well. The Highlanders showed that they had been well trained, and they went through their various dances with precision.

The entertainment was repeated On Monday evening, when there was again a large audience, which thoroughly enj4yed the efforts of the children to amuse. At the close a vote of thanks to Mr. Singleton, Miss Liptrott, and all who had taken part in the entertainment was accorded on the motion of Dr. Watkins.

MLTUAL IMPROVEMENT 50C1ETY....." Worksrs without work" was the subject under discussion at the meeting of the Bible and Mutual Improvement Class on Monday night. WESLEYAN DAY SCHOOL ENTERTAINMENT.—A succers- ful entertainment was given in the Wesleyan day school on Wednesday evening, and it was presided over by Mr. W. Clarke. The first part of the programme consisted of the performance of the cantata " Father Time and Children," and was given in a most creditable manner. Miscellaneous pieces were given in the second portion of tne programme, and included the part song " Sweet and Low," action song Paddy Blake of Bally Blarney," taken part in by 13 bays ; Japenese parasol drill, and a performance by a number of boys dressed as nigger minstrels. The prizes awarded for regular attendance were distributed by the Chairman. The entertainment was greatly appreciated by the large audience present.

FOOTBALL.

STOCKTON HEATH RESERVE V. OUGHTRINGTON PARK RESERVE—PIayed at Stockton Heath on Saturday. The round was in a very sad condition after the recent heavy storms. Stockton Heath took advantage of the wind and pressed continually, and the visiting backs being pressed, one kicked through his own goal. Soon after Andrews scored the second point for the homesters the goalkeeper letting the ball slip through his hands, half-time arriving with the score :—Stockton Heath, two ; Oughtrington, MI. The homesters held the upper hand during the second half, Andrews and Kimkerley adding goals, the whistle going for time with the score four g_als to none in favour of Stockton Heath.

A FennLs- DISPUTE.

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday, before his Honour Judge Wynne Ffoulkes, Martha Winstantley, High-st., Newton. plumber and decor- ator, sued Margaret Jones, 10, Northfield-square, Leeds, fcr £43 193. 41., the balance owing on ac- count, for money paid on her behalf. There was also a counterclaim for £35 194. 4d. for money al- leged to be owing by plaintiff to defendant for rents collected. The plaintiff and defendants Were relatives, and the case was one in which family matters were frequently brought in.—Mr. W. J. Forshaw represented the plaintiff, and Mr. A. Browne the defendant. At the commencement of the case the two solicitors went through the claim and counterclaim respectively, in order to mark those items in accounts admitted and those dis- puted. It appeared that plaintiff was occupier of a shop in Newton of which the plaintiff was the owner and that she owed certain rent for the shop, and had also collected certain other rents from other tenants in the adjoining property. The counter- claim was for amounts over and above those ad- mitted by the plaintiff as having been received by her on behalf of the defendant. Witnesses were called on both sides, and ultimately his Honour gave judgment for the claim for .£6 104., and for the counterclaim for £3O 10s.

On Tuesday an inquest !was held at the Welling- ton. Hotel, Earlestown by Mr. S. Brighouse coroner on the body of a girl named Henrietta Thompson, 12 years of age, daughter of Levi Thompson, a machineman at the Viaduct Works, and living at 12 Booth-street, Earlestown, who died on Sunday morning, From the evidence it appeared that on Saturday the girl was taken ill and put to bed. She had previously complainedfof feeling unwell, but was able to go about. At five o'clock on Sunday morning, the father and mother noticed that she was very ill, and sent for a medical man who was unable to atiend. Before another doctor could be brought, the child expired at 10 minutes to six. A ' verdict from "Natural causes," was returned.

ACCIDENT AT THE VULCAN FOUNDRY. On Thursday shortly before one o'clock, a fitt sr 11 in the employ of the Vulcan Foundry Co., named Hermon Williams, living at 59, High-street, New- ton met with a rather serious accident. It appears four loaded waggons were being put off the London and North-Western Company's line into the Vulcan Fountry yard, when owing to the greasy condition of the rails, the men were unable to control them by means of the brakes, and they ran with great force into the doors of what is known as the tender shop. The doors were flung violently back, and Williams who unfortunately happened to be in the way was struck with great force and knocked to the ground. He was rendered unconscious, and on Dr. Mounsey arriving, he found the man to be suffering from injury to the brain. He was removed home on the works ambulance litter, and is at present in a very critical condition. ' EARLESTOWN AND DISTRICT MEDICAL AID

THE DISTRICT COUNCIL CLERKSHIP.

The Sub-Committee appointed to go through the applications for the position of clerk to the Newton DiArict Council, sat again on Monday afternoon. Meeting at two o'clock they considered the applica- tions of the 55 candidates who were left in, but when they dissolved at four o'clock they had only succeeded in reducing the number to 33. The com- mittee admit the great difficulty in making a selec- tion, owing to the excellency of the candidates, and a further meeting will be held.

FIR-E AT EARLESTOWN.

About 2 15 on Thursday morning P.C. Green,who was on duty in Bridge-st., discovered that a fire had broken out in the room over the premises oc- cupied by Mr. H. Matthews, hairdresser, Bridge-st., and the Parr's Banking Co. The room is used as an office by Mr. W. Beesley, architect, of Warring- ton,and at 5 30 on Wednesday evening,Samuel Dring and Harold Hopwood, apprentices to Mr. Beesley, locked the office up leaving a good fire in the grate. The hearthstone was cracked in three places, and slightly opened,, and it is supposed that some hot ashes have fallen out of the grate, and getting through the crevices in the hearth have fallen on the floor joists beneath, and these being very dry have soon ignited. The flames have gradually crept along, and in their course badly burned the doors and floors, and destroyed a number of plans and other valuable papers. The hearth losing its sup• port by reason of the burning of the joists, fell through into the bank premises, but no damage was caused there by the fire. P.C. Green raised an alarm, and Inspector Morgan and P.C.'s Schofield and Burns were quickly on the spot, and by means of buckets of water endeavoured to quench the flames. The fire, however, had got a good hold, and the Viaduct Works fire brigade was sent for. The engine and brigade arrived about three o'clock, and a good supply of water being available, they were able to extinguish the flames by about four o'clock. Mr. Harwood and Mr. Baxter were in charge of the fire brigade. The damage is esti- mated at £2OO, covered by insurance in the County Fire Office.

NEWTON AND EARLESTOWN CONSERVATIVE CLUB.

The second of the winter series of smoking con- certs promoted by the committee of the above club was held in the large billiard-room on Friday night. Mr. R. Stone, J.P., presided, and besides a large number of members there were present—Messrs. C. B. F. Borron, W. Thompson, R. Stuart, and Dr.. Handysicle. The Chairman said he had not the pleasure of attending the first smoking concert,but he had no doubt that they were then congratulated upon the magnificent victory which they had achieved at the last Parliamentary election. He did not see any harm in indulging in those congratula- tions again, for it was a magnificent victory, not only in that division, but throughout the country in general. In the interval between the sitting of the last Parliament,and until the next,several very momentous questions had arisen,more particularly the Eastern question, and the whole c3untry had congratulated itself that at the last election it had returned a Unionist Government with a man like Lord Salisbury at its head as Foreign Secretary, and felt that they had in him a man of backbone,backed up by a Cabinet animated with sentiments in ac- cord with his own. Lord Rosebery might also be a man of backbone, but he had the misfortune not to be backed up by a Cabinet who supported his views.and it was shown that that Cabinet had such an influence over him that he he had not the courage of his own convictions. in Mr. Chamberlain, the Colonial Secretary, they had a statesman who was not satified merely with talking of the federation of this Empire with its colonies,but who was work- ing to bring his ideas to a satisfactory issue. Through his policy he (Mr. Stone) thought they would have much better trade. (Applause.) An excellent programme of songs, humorous solos, whistling solos, violin and pianoforte solos was submitted with ability by Messrs.T.Jukes,S. Swann, J. Howard, Glover, Alcock, C. H. Lea, E. Leyland, and G. Wright" At the close a vote of thanks to the chairman and the artistes was proposed and se- conded, and heartily accorded.

===== 0002601_18951214_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EARLESTOWN WESLEYAN BAZAAR.

The opening ceremony on Friday in connection with the bazaar promoted by the Wesleyan body, and held in the Town Hall, Earlestown was per- formed by Mr. J. W. Emmett, J.P. The Rev. J. Bartley presided. and there were present on the platform, Rev. E. R. Davies, Messrs. P. Parr, C. W. Emmett, S. Barrett and J. Gooding. Mr. Emmett,on rising to open the bazaar, said he found himself in a novel position. He hardly knew what to say. (Laughter.) Had it been a District Council meeting, he would have felt more at home, although little breezes did occasionally take place there. (Laughter.) The object of hold- ing that bazaar was to raise a fund to wipe off an old debt, and improve the ventilation,&c.,of the church. He proceeded to give a resume of the work done by the Wesleyan connection during the last 28 years, referring to the lack of school accommodation that then existed and to the provision for elder scholars made by the con- nexion. He then spoke of the building of the new church at a cost of £5,000, a good part of which had been reduced by the proceeds of the opening service,and the holding of bazaars. A year or two ago, the young singers thought that they would sing better if they had a new organ, and this was provided, and altogether they had spent something like £7,000 during the last 28 years. He mentioned these matters to show what the old folks had done—(laughter and applause)—and he trusted that the younger generation would go and de likewise. They wanted between £3OO and £4OO, and to judge from the takings of the previous day, which, he was informed, had amounted to £l2l 10s. ; a good beginning had been effected. He had great pleasure in declaring the bazaar open, and he might tell them that as a result of a few subscriptions, to which he himself had subscribed, he had to band over to the bazaar, the sum of £56. (Applause.) On the motion of Mr. Burgess, seconded by Mr. Gooding cordial votes of thanks were accorded to Mr. Emmett and the Chairman. The sale was afterwards vigorously proceeded with, and at the end of the day's business the sum of £125 had been realised. Or. Saturday the bazaar was opened by the Rev. J. Bartley,the Chairman on this occasion being the Rev.H. S.Sandford.During the afternoon the hall was at times in- conveniently packed and as theday advanced the stalls wore an empty appearance, one or two of them being almost denuded of their contents. The doll competition which consisted of dolls dressed entirely by young men, and representing some trade or profession, came in for plenty of attention, something like a dozen young men having sent in some excellent speciments of their handi- work, The winner of the first prize was Mr. W.C.Caw- ley, whose doll represented a cleverly dressed butcher, and Mr. Torrance for a realistic pit brow girl with trow- sets and clogs, obtained the second prize. The others were really meritorious specimens, but those mentioned were undoubtedly the best. Of the side shows that cal- ling for most praise, and which certainly received the greatest amount of attention and eulogiums from the public was the wak works of living figures. This 'was without doubt most cleverly managed, the figures pre- serving their gravity in a remarkable manner, notwith- standing the hearty outbursts of laughter from the audience. Mr. W. Clare, as showman and guide, de- serves every praise for his ingenuity, and the excellent way in which he described the figures, assisted by Mr.F. Howarth (his man Joe). The half-hour concerts were also well patronised, Mr. E. Leyland having got togs- ' ther an excellent company of artistes. On Saturday afternoon a children's concert was given by the scholars of the Sunday School, with Miss Isa Blair as accompanist, and in the evening a concert was given, at which Miss Sunworth accompanied. A humorous entertainment was also given by Mr. A. McAllister, of Liverpool, entitled " Virginia up to late," assisted by his younr, pupil Miss Ada Craig. Edison's phonograph came in for a large share of attention, and Professor J. Howard Barker operated on subjects who desired advice on love, matrimony, &c. Everybody throughout the whole affair worked with a will to ensure success, in fact to use a quotation given in the handbook " Their unanimity was wonderful." The duties of the Secre- taries were no doubt of an arduous character, but their reward and that of the committee who worked with them, will be found in the handsome sum realised dur- ing the three days, which amounted to over £327. We may mention that in addition to those mentioned last week the bazaar has been supported by the follow- ing :--.Messrs. T. T. Vicars, The Sankey Sugar Co., Mr. R. Pilkington, J.P., Mr. G. W. Rawlins, J.P., Mr Joshua Johnson, Mr. W. Thompson, Mr. E. J. Grange, &c.

HOLY TRINITY V. BEIVSRY A.F.C.—This match was played on Saturday, on the ground of the former. The ground was in a very wet and sloppy condition. Trinity won the toss, and elected to play the first half against the wind. The Bewseyites at once cprnmenoed to make things lively, and E. Rigby scored soon after the start. T. Harbourne shortly afterwards added another. This was all the scoring up to about twenty minutes after half time, when Trinity scored their first and only goal, and Bewsey ran out winners by two goals to one. Bewsey throughout played the better game, and Rigby and Leask had hard lines in not adding to the score for them.

Albert Arthur Broadbridee was sentenced to four months' hard labour at the London Sessions on Tuesday for stealing money at a house which he visited In con- nection with his duties as a coroner's officer.

PARISH COUNCIL MRETING.—At the meeting of the Run• corn Rural District Council on Tuesday, the minutes of a meeting of the Parish Council of Frodsham Lordship. held on the 9th lust, Mr. Charles Reynolds, J.P., presid- ing, were read. They showed that a letter was sub- mitted from Mr. Ockleston with respect to the insanitary condition of his premises consequent, as he alleged,upen no proper water supply and defective drainage. A letter was also read from the Medical Officer of Health in sallies that gentleman stated that there was not a proper water supply at the house mentioned,and that the water closet had had no proper means cf flushing, and was defective in construction and working. Ho had written to Mr. Ockleston who had promised to have his recommenda- tions attended to. The Chairman reported an interview be' had had with D,. Ricketts with reference to the wells at Newton, but without any satisfactory result,and also LO the present time he was in correspondence with the water engineer of the Corporation of Liverpool as to the terms of a supply from the Vyrnwy mains. He had not anything definite to lay before the committee. It was agreed that the Chairman slriuld continue his corres- pondence with the Liverpool Corporation with a view oe ascertaining definitely on what terms they would be prepared to afford a water supply. It was resolved that Mr. Diggle be instructed to prepare a rough esti- mate of the cost of extending the main sewer from Five Crosses to the boundary of the township on the' Newton side ; also that the Clerk write to Mr. Ocklestort saying the Council had under consideration the question of providing a water supply and extensi na of the sewage system, but at the same time requesting that he would carry out the recommendations of the Medical Officer of Health.—The minutes were confirmed by the Rural Council.

NO STOMACH MEDICINE, NO ELECTRIC- ITY. Sent in envolope 0,- receipt of two stamps for postage A. J. Lksw. 9E-93. G• t Russell-street. London. W.O A BOOK FOR MARR

The information contained in this book ought to be known by every married woman, awl it willnot harm the unmarriep to read. The book is conveniently divided into 12 chapters The first chapter treats of the changes at puberty, or when a girl becomes a woman. The second chapter treats of marriage from a doctor's standpoint ; points out the best age or marriage, and who should have children and who not, and furnishes useful information that one can ordinarily get only from an intelligent doctor. The third chapter treats of the marriage of blood relations; and condemns such marriages as a rule. Chapter four treats of the signs of pregnancy. The fifth chapter tells how a woman shotild live during the pregnant state. The slate chapter treats f mishaps and how to avoid them. The seventh chapter treats of maternal impressions and shows that birthmarks are not due to longings on the part of the mother, 13... t rather to her poor health. The eighth chapter teaches how to have easy confinements. Certain people believe that worn en should bring forth in pain and trouble, but the hygienic physician says that co nfinements can be made comparatively easy if certain rules are obeyed ; these rules are given. The ninth chapter treats of the proper management of confinement until the baby is born. The tenth chapter tells how to treat he mother till she is up and about again. The eleventh Chapter treats of sterility ; gives the main causes of it, how thesemay be overcome and children result. The last chapter treats of the "change," a most important article for all women over forty. The book is full of useful information and no book Is written which goes so thoroughly into matters elating to married women. Some may think too much is told ; such can srarcely be the case, for knowledge is power and the means et attaining happiness. The book can be ha from Dr. T. R.
